PEN KETH.

ORDER OF DRUIDS.—PENKETH UNITED LODGE.—At the monthly meeting of the above lodge, the members were honoured by a visit from two of the district officers, namely, Bro. J. Bibby, Deputy District Grand Master, and Bro. David Houghton, D.C.S. Bro. H. Hughes vacated the N.G.A.'s chair in favour of Bro. Bibby, and Bro. T. Broom occupied the vice-chair. Bros. NV. Wh tfield and H. Mai nley,the Secretary and Treasurer, were also presQnt, and loege officers. Several newmem- bers were Initiated,being introduced by Bro.W.Dean,who has been instrumental in securing many new members,' and has in this way earned the, emblem of the Society. At the conclusion of the Lodge business,Bro. H. Mainley moved that the thanks of the meeting be tendered to the district officers for their visit. Bro. Whitfield seconded and it was unanimously carried. Bro. Bibby responded and complimented the ledge on the sustained progress that was being made, and congratulated Bro. Dean on his success in securing new members. The lodge was closed.

The Examiner, Friday, May 15, 1903. FASHIONS OF TO-DAY. THE NIGHT OF THE CRIME.

women's walk, never a ”raceful movement, has gone from bad to worse. Heels are higher, and shoes tighter and the wearers more closely resemble the rope walker when4balancing himself on his precarious footing than anything else. If such misguided mortals could once experience the comfort and delight of a broad soled, fairly-heeled boot or shoe, they would possibly need considerable persuasion ere returning to the torturous footgear they are endeavouring to walk in just now. With the modish grey toilette, grey shoes and hose are worn, but such a whim is costly, as with a grey gown only can they be donned. Moreover they must match the gown in tint precisely, or the effect will be wrong. Tan shoes and hose are very much more useful and unlike grey, have not the tmpleasing habit of mak- ing the foot appear larger than it really is. For outdoor wear, buckled shoes are quite taking the place of the laced or buttoned varieties. These are made with a big flop, and the large buckle is usually in steel or paste.

Upon hygienic grounds the paper handkerchief is no doubt preferable to the washable linen kind, but so far the Japanese custom of using paper handkerchiefs has not made much progress in Europe. Almost anything can now be made of paper, and some beautiful speci- mens of these handkerchiefs were to be seen at the recent Chemists Exhibition in London. They cost about one farthing each the paper being of an exceedingly i soft and fine texture. It s said that in a few years time we shall be wearing stockings made of woven paper, but this prospect seems less probable than a pos- sible general . adoption of the paper handkerchief. _ _ _

The-balloon sleeve was incOnrenient enough, but in Its most inflated day it was hardly the nuisance the sleeve of the moment"proves to be. More and more material is crowded into the space between elbow and wrist, often, enough to make an entire blouse, and the number of accidents caused through this useless append- age must be enormous. To watch a modish woman endeavour to pass anything at table is an entertainment in itself. Two hands are necessary, one to accomplish the deed, the other to hold the sleeve up. The second sleeve meanwhile is usually dipping into a plate, or overturning a wine glass, when confusion reigns supreme. And there is more to come, according to leading lights in Vienna and Paris._

Some women always leave the choice ot new toilettes to the very last possible minute, only to find that they are seziously incon- venienced by the delay in the execution thereof, as the milliners and dressmakers have every minue of their time occupied, for so many people seem to arrive simul- taneously at the conclusion that new gowns and hats are an immediate necessity. Lost opportunities lie in the showery or stormy days of early spring, when one has plenty of time to leisurely decide how and in what style they will appear in the days of brilliant sunshine.

Some of this season's tailor-made gowns have the skirt cut to clear the ground all the way round—which, of course, is a sensible plan; but should not be worn by those women who possess ugly feet or who are unable to wear shoes or boots which are of a good shape and fit perfectly. A PRETTY TOILETTE

La Mode still smiles approvingly on should be adopted. The pretty toilette sketched shows a skirt made in the latest style with gaugings round the waist, where a deep and well-fitting broad band makes the waist look neat and trim. The lower part Of the skirt also shows gaugings introduced at intervals, while it flows in full, soft folds round the feet. The bodice is finished by a deep cape of ecru lace finished at the neck by bands- of embroidery. The material employed is blue canvas over green silk, a touch of green appearing at the neck and in the embroidery, while a green enamel buckle fastens the broad silk waistband. A rather picturesque hat trimmed with lace and some green tulle completes an elegant and exceedingly smart toilette.

Foulard is a fabric which every summer reappears in an improved form, and, like tile blouse, seems to be always with us, for its useful and ornamental qualities are recog; nised by all. Those displayed this season are particularly fine and soft, and are almost as adaptable as mousselines to the frillings, pleatings, and gaugings which the present fashions demand. There are some charming combinations of colours employed in the patterns, which are printed on plain or shot grounds, while blurred chine or vompadour patterns are also much in vogue.

===== 0002601_19030515_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
A MORNING BLOUSE,

The blouse illustrated is suitable for morning wear, and is quite the latest thing. It is made of holland in a deep ecru or tea- tint in slip form, fastening down the back with a broad box-pleat in front, finished off at the throat with a small lace collar and cravat tied in a bow in front. The sleeves widen, of course, towards the hand, where they are finished by a deep wristlet covered with lace, which reaches some distance up the arm, raises the fulness, and prevents it drooping over the hand as before, the effect being much prettier and less floppy than of yore. Sometimes these coarse linen crash or holland blouses are trimmed with pure white Irish crochet or black and white em- broidery. The same materials are also used in the construction of smart little coats or boleros for morning wear over blouses. The smartest of these are lined with silk, and are trimmed with filet lace or coarse crochet braiding. These coats are made quite short, reaching to the waist-line, or in some instances a little above. it. Filet lace, it should be noted, is newer than guipure, and it looks very well with all the most fashionable materials. If is often used for capes or berthes, and is sometimes finished at the edge with another and quite different kind of lace.

A MODISH 'TOQUE.

The toque sketched is designed for after- noon wear, and is made of gauged pink tulle, which covers the upturned brim, and is ornamented in front by a black osprey, which is caught by a jet cabochon. The crown is covered with tiny banksia roses massed close together. The newest hats are of the plate order, and are absolutely flat as to crown, and trimming relying alone upon how they are worn to give them a cachet. Here it is that the Frenchwoman excels, and the English- woman, alas! too often fails—for -the former has an intuition how to place her hat on her head at just the right angle to produce a smart effect; while the latter unfor- tunately seems to think that so long as she puts her hat on that is all that should be expected of her! This season especially care must be taken in the putting on of the most up-to-date hats, or they will prove an utter and complete failure. In many instances the brim is turned sharply back from the face, and it requires much careful study before a glass to arrive at the best results, for the hat must not project too far or too little over the face, The French maletot sailor hat in likely to be well and much worn during summer, and is certainly a very smart and simple style of hati CoguErrEl

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Two of the former Queens of Scotland were the daughters of the King of Demnark so that Queen Alexandra is not the first but the third Danish Princess to preside over the hospitalities of Holyrood Palace. The Queen of James VI. of Scotland was a daughter of Frederick IT. and it was whilst staying at Holyrood that they received the news that they were King and Queen of Great Britain. That was 300 years ago, as it was on the 24th March 1603 that James VI. succeeded as James I. to the throne of England. The tercentenary has passed almost unnoticed, but Queen Alexandra could hardly have failed to be reminded that 300 years ago a Princess of her own Royal dynasty was mistress of Holyrood. The other Danish Princess to occupy this position was a daughter of Christian I. of Denmark, and the wife of James 111. and it was in consequence of this union that Scotland acquired the Orkney and Shetland Islands.

The Duke of Buccleuch's seat, Dalkeith Palace,where their Majesties have been staying, is one of three ducal mansions styled palaces. It is commonly known as Dalkeith House, but there has beena Palace on the site THE Po from the time when James IV was King of Scotland. The other two are Hamilton Palace,the seat of the Duke of Hamilton, and Blenheim Palace the seat of ,the Duke of Marlborough. The Duke of Marlborough is Heredi- tary Keeper of Holyrood Palace,and has therefore been I prominently engaged in the reception of the King and : Queen at the Scottish capital. The Duke and Duchess of Marlborough will reside mostly at Blenheim during the summer, as they have given up Warwick House, their town residence. which the Duke has hitherto , leased from Lady Warwick. The new mansion which I the Duke of Marlborough is having built in London is _ _

ANEMIC WOMEN NOTE! - Bile Beans area Certain Cure !

The following case illustrates forcibly the curative properties of Chas. Forde's Bile Beans in cases of anwmia :—Miss Clara Ryan, of 231, Canterbury-road, New Brompton, Kent, suffered for years from a number of ailments, all of which arose out of one root trouble "My heart was very weak," she says, "and I was troubled with indigestion and sleeplessness. Headache tortured me constantly, and I was sick, and vomited after nearly every meal. Most frequently when I arose in the morning had, an attack of palpi- tation, which increased till I fainted. I could never get upstairs without someone to assist me, or by taking a couple of steps and then sitting and resting. I had a doctor attending me, then I became an out-patient at St. Bartholomew's Hospital, Chatham, but no benefit resulted.

" I was .one day serving a lady in my father's shop, when she, noticing my pale, amemic condition' told me she had found Bile Beans an excellent cure. I took Bile Beans after that, and almost at once began to improve. My appetite returned, the pains left me, and my colour returned. After I had taken a few boxes, I was thoroughly restored to health,and I have continued to be so."

Chas. Forde's Bile Beans may be had from all medicine vendors, at is. lid. or 2s. 9d. per box. They are the finest family medicine knovna. They do not merely purge, giving temporary relief only like the old-fashioned so-called remedies of forty or fifty years ago. They act directly on the liver and digestive organs, steengthening and stimulating them to perform their natural duties. They produce a gentle action on the bowels, curing or preventing constipation, cleansing the stomach and ridding the system of all impurities. Do not be misled by claims of half a hundred pills in the box, where probably four to six constitute a dose, and the doses cannot be discontinued, One Bile Bean is one dose. They can be discontinued after the cure is effected; they are purely vegetable; they contain no harmful drugs, and they are the safest family medicine.

THE BARROW STEAM NAVIGATION COMPANY announce the opening of their Barrow and Douglas ser- vice for the season on May 29th. They have added to their fleet the magnificent express steamer "Duchess of 13ucc1euch," which has been specially fitted up with all the best and latest improvements for the comfort and accommodation of passengers, and it is believed she will greatly add to the popularity of the Barrow route. This steamer will in conjunction with the well-known steamer "Duchess of Devonshire" conduct the services of the Barrow Co.'s Isle of Man trade during the coming season. Passengers travelling by the Barrow route can combine a visit to the English Lakes, with their Isle of Man tour, they can break their journey at Ulverston, take a cheap circular tour ticket, resuming their 'journey to Douglas at their con*Ohience.

THE AMERICA CUP DEFENDER. THE RELIANCE/

IkiE RELIANCE.

The :ace for the America Cup, which pro- mises to provide the sporting centre of interest this summer, wears a more promising appear- ance for Great Britain than it has done for 'Many years, despite the serious accident to Shamrock 111. a month ago. She, however, has been refitted With the most commendable Promptitude, and is once more "tuning up". before her voyage across the Atlantic. Every time that she is seen out she gives more satis- faction to the critics, who pronounce her a marvellously good boat, and she is now not a bit the worse for the catastrophe at Wey- mouth. The Reliance, as the defender and champion of America has been Christened, hp.s, on the other hand, met with considerable hostile criticism across the water, and though a few hail her as the finest yacht ever built, the vast majority have hardly a good word for her. However, her trials with the Col- umbia will soon prove whether she is as bad as she is painted or whether she is another success for the Herreshofs, the great Ameri- can yacht builders. Her dimensions are—r4l feet in length and 25 feet io inches in the beam, with a draught of 19 feet 6 inches. In length and beam she is the largest defender since the Vigilant, and the lead in her keel weighs a hundred tons. The proportion of the hull under water is the smallest ever seen in a yacht of this size, whilq the hull is fully ten feet longer than that of either the Colum- bia or the s(onstitution, and quite six inches broader than in the case of the latter. Still She displaces less water and shows less wetted 'surface than either! However, as things stand at the moment, despite accidents, English Prospects look brighter than .they have done for a very long while.

The King and Queen left London on Monday morning from King's Cross, travelling to Edinburgh by the East Coast route, and reached Edinburgh in the evening. THE FIRE EXHIBITION AT EARL'S COURT.

The International Fire Exhibition, which .I'as opened at Earl's Court last week by the 01(1 Duke of Cambridge, is an exhibition ol than ordinary interest to everybody. It ltsilfull of various fire-engines and escapes from oldest engine extant, built in 1570, the storic engine which used to protect Windsor astle, and the hand-engines used to quell the

Great Fire of London, down to the most rnodern motor fire-engine, built by Messrs. Merryweather, which can go thirty miles an cur and pump four hundred gallons of water a minute, and a fifty-two foot motor fire- escape, with a chemical engine, by the same brill. In the Empress Theatre there is de- Picted a most thrilling scene of stupendous realism. It is a London street and a night Ire• The alarm is sounded, and up dash the engines. The hoses are got to work, the escapes rescue the inmates, and the fire, which bursts into most realistic flames, is gradually tinguished before the eyes of the audience. -Che whole thing is most excellently done and is highly realistic. The rest of the grounds are as usual filled up by various side-shows, Which help to pass a few hours pleasantly, and taxi's Court promises to be in for another suc- cessful season. The actual exhibition itself is of considerably more than usual interest to 'the average individual.

THE ORITA.

Pessimists have during the last year uttered many prophecies about the decadence of British shipping and the advance of America. These, however, have hardly been borne out by events. Last week, for instance, there was a tremendous slump in New York in the Shares of the Pierpont Morgan Shipping Trust, Which is not proving the success anticipated, as the British lines are holding their own well against their opponents. Another point of interest in this connection was the maiden viaYage of the Pacific Steam Navigation Com- Pany's new steamer Orita, which commenced last week. She sails in the Orient Pacific line

BETWEEN ENGLAND AND AUSTRALIA. NOW ON

HER MAIDEN VOYAGE

to Australia, and is the largest vessel trading 'between England and Australia under the tritish flag, The Orita was built by Messrs. liarland and Wolff, of Belfast, ,who took such a„ Prominent part in the recent controversy. She is a twin-screw vessel of 9,23 i tons gross _register, .485 feet long, and 58 feet in the k'ani. She has accommodation for rso first c.la-ss, 125 second class. and „a large number of third class passengers; and her saloons and cabins are maznificently fitted. :lany of the latter are fitted on the .Bibby patent tandem .sYstern, which is now b:.Tiniting to come into general use. Altogether, the Orita is a worthy addition to the British mercantile service, and sh°\v's that we are not falling bAind i race of competition

DALKEITH HOUSE

they are making their bend-quarters owing to the insanitary state of Holyrood Pnlace, the historic home of the Scottish Kings. On Tuesday Edinburgh was traversed on the way to Holyrood, the route being lined by ten thousand soldiers. At Holy- rood in the foremoon a levee was- held by the King. and in the afternoon a drawing- room by the Queen, at which some six hundred presentations were made. The prin- cipal event of Wednesday is the opening of the new City Hospital at Colinton Mains, and on Thursday their Majesties visit Glasgow. There the King has promised to lay the foundation stone of the new Technical College, and the magnificent Municipal Buildings, the Art Galleries of the University will be visited. The King and Queen return to town at the end of the week after what is certain to be a record week in the annals of Scotland.

THE MOAT rArad MYSTERY.

The Moat farm case is advancing slo%‘ly stcp by step, and, as it develops, public interest seems to grow with it. At one time it appeared as though no body was likely to be found, and public excitement was decidedly on the wane, when a skeleton was dug -up and sworn to as Miss Holland by several witness- • , Then the question Was as to the • cause of death, and it was thought that the medical authori- ties of the Home Office might find the utmost diffi- culty in arriving at a conclusion. Dr. - Pepper, whose name is k 30 familiarowing to his official connection with many famous cases, was, how- ever, able to discover it at once. In the skull just above DR. A. J. PEPPER, WHO the left eve he DISCOVERED THE BULLET. found a bullet, which had entered from behind the right ear, and could not of itself have failed to cause death. The wound, Dr. Pepper considers, could not have been self- inflicted from the direction taken by the bullet. • These discoveries have again centred the eye of the public upon the mysterious lonely farm in Essex, and the inquest and the pro- ceedings before the magistrates have been most eagerly followed. Dougal, who stood under remand on a charge of forgery, has been forniglly charged with murdering Miss Holland on May 19th. 1899, and he has been: remanded upon this fresh count as -well.

VAUXHALL BRIDGE.

Every Londoner has been aware for some. time that Vauxhall Bridge has been in a process of evolution, and it would surprise some to learn that the . design only conies up before the I.ondon County Council , this week for a final decision. This design, however. which is, of course, under the auspices of the Bridges Committee, only affects the artistic treatment of the upper part, as the prelimi- nary or engineering design was approved some months ago. The idea Pn developing the scheme has been to depend largely upon the simplicity of the engineering lines of the bridge, and thus to secure dignity by em- phasising these, treating the approaches in a substantial manner. In the design shown in the picture, which is the one originally drawn by Mr. W. F. Riley, the County Council's architect, . assisted' by Mr. Fitzrnaurice, the

engineer, an attempt has been made to intro duce a Parisian ici,ture in the —pylons,' which have never fcirred a part of a Londor bridge. These oblong pylons are carried higt above the stone balustrades at the entrance of the bridge, and they are introduced not only to give dignity to the appearance, but also to lend additnt,l strength to the struc- ture. T:nis desir.zr, sembles in many ways the Alexander 111. Bridge across the Seine, where, however, the pylons are treated more ornamentally. But the Bridges Committee while accepting the design, and being favour- ably impressed by Ole pylons, .hgves decided not to adopt them as they would entail the additional cost of Lio,ooo. Thus the design will go to the L.C.C. this week shorn of the pylons; and that is likely to be the plan Adopted:

LOCAL SHEPHERDRY.

Half-yearly Meeting of the Lancashire and Adjacent Counties Conference.

The members of the Lancashire and Adjacent Counties Conference of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds (Ashton Unity) held their third half-yearly meeting on Saturday at the Literary Institute Sandbach. The President, Bro. W. H. Sugden, presided, and the attend- ance included Bros. L. Weir (vice-president), J. Parr, W. Eckersly, P. Edgar W. Hall, T. Mellor, and J. Adams (Warrington); P. Ball (St. Helens); S. 'Judd, C. W. Healey, W. London, and T. Jones (Golborne) ; with Bro. J. Ashcroft, secretary pro tem.; and Bro. Degnam, treasurer.

The President, in opening the proceedings, congratu- lated the meeting upon the manner in which their dis- trict* had carried out the suggestions of the conference, and, continuing, he said that part of the business of that meeting was to discuss the propositions to the A.M.C. of alterations in the rules. Personally, he did not agree with some of the propositions, and one of these was that the assistant secretary should go to the annual meeting, and that in addition to his expenses he should be paid 5s more than the other members while there. He contended that 10s. per day was sufficient. Another amendment proposed was that in future the treasuper should be considered an officer of the order. To this be also objected, for it would mean that the treasurer should attend the Board meetings and this would entail considerable expense. The question of "wet-rents" would also be considered by the A.M.C., a proposition having been made to the effect "that it shall be illegal for any lodge or district to pay for tha place of meeting by the purchase of refresh- ments." He was a "dry-renter" and he had always re- iarded " wet-rents "as an evil. His lodge met in a Sunday schoolroom. Another proposition upon which the conference had to pass an opinion was that a land- lord of a public-house where a lodge met was not a fit and proper person to be 'entrusted with the duties of treasurer of a lodge. In his districtalioensed victualler was one of the most enthusiastic members of the society. That gentleman held the position of treasurer to a lodge and was it right that stioh a valuable officer, as he had proved himself to be, shouldbs requested to give up his office because he was aipublican ? He suggested that!an amendmont should be made to the effect that in future the landlord of a public-house where a lodge met could not be elected to the position of treasurer of that lodge.

ELECTION OF SECRETARY.

Bro. J. H. Gartside was elected to the position of secretary on the motion of Bro. Brown, of Chorley, seconded by,Bro. Brookes, of Manchester.

THE QUESTION OF MEETINGS AT PUBLIC-HOUSES

The proposition to the A.M.C. that the treasurer should be an ex-otficio member of the Board of Manage- ment was considered, and the Conference registered their disapproval of it. The next proposition was " that the host or hostess of a licensed house where a lodge or district meets shall not be eligible for election as treasurer for such lodge or district."

Bro. Smith proposed, and Bro. Brookes seconded,that they should oppose the opposition, it being remarked that publicans hada great influence on their customers in making them members of the society. Bro. Edgar regarded the proposition to the A.M.C• as an insult to a body of men who could be quite as honourable as any others. Bro. Billiuge moved as an amendment that the pro- positionshould be supported, and this was seconded. Bro. Edgar thereupon moved as a rider, that such a rule should only apply in case of a vacancy in future. Bro. Turner seconded, and the ridet was carried by a large majority. It was unanimously decided to support the proposi- tions that widows and orphans of members could be granted relief. With regard to the proposition that it should be illegal for any lodge or district to pay for the 'place of meeting by the purchase of refreshments. • Bro. Turner moved that it should be supported, Bro. W. Lea seconding. Bro. Edgar thought that the existing conditions should not be disturbed, but that the matter should be left to the discretion of the various lodges. He proposed as an amendment that it should not be supported. . . _

Bro. Smith seconded, and the amendment was carried by 14 votes to eleven. It was decided to hold the next mooting at Golborne.

RUGBY FOOTBALL. NORTHERN UNION MEETING.

The Northern Union Committee met on Tuesday, Mr. Clifford in the chair. It was decided that the Manchester headquarters should be the Grosvenor Hotel, Deansgate. Cumberland County Union were granted £5O. The Halifax Club were allowed to present medals to five reserve players and others. Five pounds was donated Cheshire County Union for medals for the Junior Com- petition. Messrs Clifford, Smith, Harrison, Cooke,. Houghton, and Platt were appointed to revise rules and bylaws.

THE anticipated do- THE PROTECTIONISTS monstration from AND THE BUDGET. the Protectionists on Tuesday was limited to a protest from Mr. JAMES LOWTHEE• The simple fact is that in consequence of the manner in which the business of the House vies arranged the possibility of discussing what 114 r. BALFOUR now frankly describes as "the bread tax" was excluded. Mr. LoWTHER appears to have been very angry. What he wanted was a general debate on the budget proposals, but he found himself confined to specific resolutions, which, as Oven Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNER3IAY complained, the House had never seen in print. The result of a method of proceeding which was much out of the ordinary, and which Mr. RITCHIE penitently promised not to repeat if he should be in the happy position of produz.ing a Budget next year, was that the corn duty was dropped without a word being said on the subject. The CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER pointed out that the duty may be made the subject of discussion when the Finance Bill is before the House; but, as he knows very well that is not the same thing as a general dis- cussion before the taking of the Budget resolu- tions. There is not the remotest possibility that the corn duty will now be restored. Private members cannot make taxation proposals Now that HUMPTY DUMPTY has fallen from the Protectionist wall, it would require all the strength of all the KING'S Ministersito put him up again,and unless it were in danger of imminent defeat by the Protec- tionists that is a task that the Government is not at all likely to undertake. In fact the CHAPLIN' LOWTHER party has been rather cleverly out- manoeuvred. It can now merely talk Proteetion it can make no step towards again putting it into actual practice. We shall hear no more of the corn duty after what will be merely empty discussion at the Finance Bill stage. There is an election progress at Preston. He would be a courageous candidate who stood up before a Lancashire audience to defend a Government which was pro- posing to continue a tax on bread. On the Budget resolutions Sir HENRY FOWLER raised the question of the tobacco duty. He pointed out that tobacco is not making any contribution to the cost of the war. It is, in fact paying less than when the war began. Conservative CHANCELLORS of the Ex- CHEQUER have hitherto been rather fond of fiddling with the tobacco duty. They have increased it and reduced it again and again in such a way as to make the changes at once annoying to the tobacco trade and unjust to the consumer. In 1898, as Sir HENRY FOWLER pointed out, the tobacco tax was reduced sixpence, which did not reduce the price of tobacco, but went to the tobacco manufacturers and dealers. Subsequently . there was an increase of fourpence, which left the , tax twopence below the level of the ante-war period. Why should a luxury lik3 tobacco be exempt from increased taxation when addi- tional burdens are placed on necessary articles like tea and sugar? The reply of Mr. Rfrcint is that taxation on this particular article has reached its limit, and that an increase of the tax does not produce an increase of revenue.— a statement that must have been received with a considerable amount of wonder and incredulity. The House hurried through the Budget resolu- tions with most unaccustomed speed; but there was naturally some discussion as to the amount at, which the CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER pro- poses to fix the sinking fund. This is £27,000,000, a sum which not only makes no provision for the liquidation of the new war debt, hut actually falls half-a-million short of paying the annual charge of this most recent addition to the national lia- bilities. In defending the °muse he has adopted Mr. %WHIR made a .very remarkable admission. He said it is ridiculous to suppose that the tax-

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

NEW WIDNES _MAGISTRATE.

At Widnes, on Monday, Mr. Thomas Smith, chairman of Whiston Rural District Council, resident at Bold, was sworn in as &justice of the peace in the place of Mr. T. H. Thomas by virtue of his office as chairman of the above Council. MR. G. F. ASHTON. _ -

Mr. George F. Ashton, clerk to the Runcorn Union, clerk to the Runcorn Rural District Council, &c., has passed his final examination for admittance as a solicitor. We heartily congratulate him upon this important addition to the qualifications he holds for his various public offices.

A NEGLECTFUL FATHER.

At the Runcorn Police Court on Saturday, Patrick Roach, of Runcorn, was charg3d on a warrant, with deserting his iwife and family Mr. T. Cooke, relieving °leer, stated that the wife and family had had to go to the Workhouse in consequence of desertion.—Prisoner was sent to gaol for a month with hard labour.

SERIOUS ACOIDENT TO A RUNCORN BOY.,

Thomas T-heobald, a boy of eight, living at 21, Surrey- street, Runcorn, met with a serious accident at the Top Locks on Friday evening. It seems that the gates were being locked and he was attempting to hook the flange to the post when he was caught between the two and badly crush9d. He was attended by Dr.. McLennan. Though severely internally injured, he is stated to be slowly recovering. PRESENTATION TO A FORMER RUNCORN SCHOOL NI A.STER.

AN ECHO OF THE WARRINGTON CORONATION CELEBRATIONS. Presentations to Friendly Society Representatives. ! . It seems a far cry to the Coronation celebrations of last year, but the friendly societies of the town haire not forgotten the part they . took in recognising the , auspicious event, and on Wednesda,y evening they, paid ; honour to whom honour is due when they presented Bro. George Maybury,of the Ancient Order of Foresters and Bro David Houghton of the Order of Druids while small , memento of the active part they took in organising the ' Friendly Societies Procession. Bro. Maybury it may be stated, acted as Chairman of the Friendly Societies . Council, and Bro. Houghton as honorary secretary and ' both gentlemen discharged the onerous duties with , credit to themselves and the friendly societies they re- presented. The interesting event took place in the Odd- fellows Lodge Room at the Pelican Hotel,Buttermarket- street. The presentations consisted of handsomely . framed and inscribed enlarged photographs of the re- cipients. The photograph of Bro. Maybury bore the . following inscription: Presented to Mr. Geo. A day or two ago the head,naster, Mr. Taylor, of the Higher Grade School, Moss Side, Manchester, presented to Mr. L. W. Jeacock a handsome marble timepiece, subscribed for by the teaching staff and scholars on his leaving to became headmaster of the North Crawley National School, Newport Pagnell. Mr. Jeacock was an assistant teacher at the Victoria-road Board School, Rancorn, before going to Manchester.

A Drsm/SSA.L.

At the Runrn Police Court, on Friday, before Messrs. F. J. Norman (in the chair), and E. Tinison,John Basnett, Runcorn, was charged with sleeping out and having no visible means of subsistence.—The evidence showed that twelve months ago he was sent to Upton Asylum, but had recently been discharged.—Prisoner contended he had only been discharged a fortnight ago, and as he would receive for a.couple of months an allow- ance from the Asylum authorities he could not be with- out means of subsistence.—Prisoner was given the benefit of the doubt and discharged.

ST. JOHN'S CHURCH, COMPLETION FUND.

On Tuesday evening a social gathering was held in the Mission Hall, Earlestownin connection with the above fund, the arrangement being carried out by the teachers of St. John's &day School. There was only a moder- ate attendance. Dancing was indulged in to music sup- plied by Mr. Woodcock's band, and was ke lt up until 11 o'clock. Mr. T.Hardman officiated as EARLESTOWN MAY QUEEN FESTIVAL..

The date for the observance of this festival is-fast approacffing, and every effort is being made to ensure success. All depends upon the wenther, ant it is to be hoped this will be of a propitious ciAaracter. The work of preplratiou is of a gigantic char.tcte,r, but plenty of energy is being thrown into it and nothing will be left undone by the committee to bring the affair to a successful, conclusion.

In connection with the annual Synod held at S. Helens under the auspices of the Wesleyan Connexion, religious services were held in the Wisleyan Church Earlestown on Tuesday and Wednesday evenings. On Tuesdly evening the service was conducted by the Rev. J. H. Siloll of 53uthport. John Barns, "The Band of Hope as a !news of Social Reformation." and the Rev. R. Wilkins Re" 3!l"Rsidin, as a Alants,l Discipline and Means. of Grace." gr. Sohn Allen of Birkenhead presided. CO-OPERA.TIVR DIV ID SND C IO ITS POINT. At Newton-le-Willows Police Court, on Thursday, the Earlestown Co-oparative Society were sued by two of their members, Edwin and S trail Ogden, for payment of £3 55., including £2 15s. dividend.on metallic Checks. —Mr. White, Warrington, said-the checks represented purchases made, by or on behalf of his clients.—Mr. Somers, Manchester, forthe society,tontead.ed that the circumstances pointed to the plaintiffs procuring them in an irregular manner. Mrs. Ogden told a man named Slater to dispose of them, and he offered them for sale in the town at a shilling per pound, when the legal owner could have obtained 2s. 6d. atthe offices.- Stater told a witness named Davies Mrs, Ogden had more checksthan she dare take in. He told the President of the Society, and they refused to pay Mrs. Ogden divi- dend, The bench held it unproved that the plaintiffs had obtained the checks irregularly, and the society • . must therefore pay. ST. Jo fcc's Cllrntcll, E ARLESTO WS .

The annual dongregationdtea party and entertain- ment which was held in the Mission Hall on Thursday evening was made the occasion for extending a welcome to the Rev. W. H. Ainley the newly appointed Curate, and to Nurse Brown who has taken the place- of Nurse Irwin. There were about 200 people p.raseut to tea, and after the tables were cleared, an, Interesting enter- tainment was given, the .following 'programme being submitted. Chorus "The Sailor's Chorus," St. John's Wale Voice Choir;. dialogue "Old Maids Matched" Messrs. ,Clayton, Spencer, Smith, Horridge, Croft, Clarke, Spaacer, Harris and Brown ; chorus "Welsh Air," St. John's Male Voice Choir; tableau, "The Gipsy Countess" Mrs. Wright. and Mr. Tucker ; song, Mr. Garnett ; tableau, ' Fairyland " Misses Croft, florridge and Smith ; tableau, "Scene in Spain" Mrs. Spencer and Wright, Misses Clayton. F. Hatch, Harrison and Naylor; tableau, Darkies' Wed ding" Messrs. Brown, Maudsley, Tucker, Nnttall,Garnett, Hewitt, Forster and H. Brown; glee" Slumber song' St. John's Choir.—The Rev. T. R. Mat thews presided; and expressed his gratification that they were spared to meet together once more. - The past year had on the whole been a very successful one, the parochial workhad gone on fairly well, the Sunday school had to a . large extent prpsparei, and the day schools were in a floarishing condition. lie then ex- tended, on behalf of the eongregation,a hearty welcome to Nurse Brown and to the Rev W. H. Ainley. .What they had to do was to work togethar„ to pall together, to pray together and to love together and then he did not fear the results, as he was persuaded they should all be satisfied if they worked on these lines. Mr. Ainley would find that they did not give him too much work, but that he would find out work for himself, and when a man does that he does it in his own way and does it wall. Re asked the congregation to do their part and not to expect too much from the clergy. (Applause.) Nurse Brown acknowledged the kindly welcome and promised to do her best to visit everyone in the parish. The Rev. W. H. Ainley also briefly thanked them for the kind reception they had given him and asked them to assist him in his arduous task. Mr. Pimblett and Dr. Aounsey, is wardens, also spoke, assuring both Nurse Brown and Mr. Ainley of the sympathy and the assistance of the parishioners.

RUNCORN NOTES.

LB V A CO it It Es POND ENT].

The presentation of the West Lancashire 'Cup and medals to the Runcornplavers tookplace on Wednesday 'evening at,Wilson's Hoteat was a very happy gathering and it was confidently asserted that had it not been for thedisastrous spell of bad luck in the early part of the season Runcorn would have had at least one more cup to boast of. In connection with the Runcorn Cottage Hospital Mr. Taylor suggested that next year the Club arrange a match with some proalinent team, the pro- ceeds to be devoted to the C.ELF. There is every pro- bability that the suggestion will be acted upon,and if this can be made an annual affair a portion, at all events, of the annual financial liability will be met.

On Monday night a meeting was held by the Wesleyan body , to consider the partial rebuilding of St. Paul's Church. The amount required is at least .t,1,800 and it was decided to raise . this stun by subscription. Mr. F. J. Nor nan and Mr. Taylor each promised 2100 to the fund. The "Million Guinea Scheme" severely taxed the re- sources of local Methodism but the new financial burden will, without doubt, be borne cheerfully. What local Methodist is there who would not" pinch!' him- self to scrape a copper or two together for his beloved St. Paul's?

Mr. G. F. Ashton, clerk to the Rural Council and Board of Guardians, is now a duly qualified solicitor, and, in company with others, we must congratulate him upon his success in gaining an honour which will be an important acquisition to him in . the discharge of his duties.

LOCAL BANKRUPTS APPLY FOR THEIR DISCHARGE. Hearing at the Couqty Court.

His Honour Judge &Oven RowlandsK. C. was engaged for some considerable time on Thnrsday at the Warring- ton County Court in hearing two applications for dis- charge from bankruptcy. Mr. A, Browne made both applications and the 0 ficial Receiver (Mr. Dibb) appeared in person. The first application was made on behalf of Matthew Pimlott Pownall, formerly of Brookside Farm, Great Sankey, but now residing at Burtonwood. The Oacial Receiver submitted some lengthy observ- ations in which he contended that the applicant had been guilty of certain offences under the Bankruptcy Act of not keeping proper books of ax3count.&c. Itwis a case, he said, in which he did not suggest there should be more than two years' suspension having regard to the painful character of the case. His Honour remarked that he did not know of a farmer who did keep proper books, and he had never met one who admitted he had /Made' any profits. (Laughter.) He did not, find in this instance that it was a.noffence not to keep books. , Mr. Browne pointed out that the bankruptcy was entirely due to an adverse decision against the appli- cant in an action which he took against his landlord, Captain Hughes. He --contended that the applicant could not beheld responsible for the offence brought against him by the .Official Receiver of not paying lOs. in the .£, inasmuch as he took the opinion of Mr. Hr- ridge, K.C., before bringing his action. . His Honour granted an immediate discharge and held that the applicant could not justly be held responsible for the result of the action. •

G ALL AYTRY LEADS TO ILG-HEALTH. AN ELEnRICAL' ENGINEER'S HARD CASE. Walter Treiitt Sampson. electrical engineer, Warring- ton, applied for his discharge from the bankruptcy proceedings which he and his former partner, Mr. Morton. had entered into when carrying on business of electrical engineers under the style of Morton and Sampson. Mr. Dibb reported on the whole bankruptcy, proceedings, and submitted that the assets did. not amount to 103. in the R, and that the books of account did not show the true financial position of the firm. This was a case demanding considerable sympathy, as bankrupt had. suffered from ill-health, and one which might he treated oat of the ordinary course. Mr. Browne, explained that rip to about ten years ago bankrupt masa strong healthy man, but at that time he plunged int-) the river Mersey, in order to save a lady, and was for a considerable time immersed in the water. He had since been tinder Dr. Rawling„ of Manchester, and had suffered very considerably in his health, and could not give the whole of his attention to the business, which might have been expected. He joined Mr. Morton' inpartnership, in November,l9ol, and paid £125 as his share in the capital. In the next nine months he also paid other sums of monev,amounting to , £6O or .£7O, into the firm. That was all loss, as the petition was filed in August. 1902. With regard to the books the partnership contained a proviso that the balancesheet should only be prepared once every twelve months,. and before the first year had elapsed the .peti- tion had been filed. ' His Honour said they were all asked to do their very 'best for the bankrupt, .whose gallant, conduct deserved well of them all, and whose responsibility was of a very limited .character. He granted 'him his immediate discharge, judgment to be entered up for the balance this to the creditors in respect Of the liabilities prov- able, but such judgment to be deemed to be satisfied when the creditors, with the dividend already declared and paid. have received 3s. in the pound. Mr. Dibb said that would mean about .C2O. Mr. Sampson could pay when he4otild. There was nothing binding until the Court was satisfied that he' could pay

Cycling Accident in Knutsiovd-road.

TAt about half-past one on Wadnesday afternoon Thomas Nelson, who resides at 36, Cumberland-street, Latchford, was cycling along Knutsford-road and when opposite the "Golden Lion" Inn, his machine collaprd, throwing him violently to the ground. He was ptcked up and conveyed to the Infirmary in a cab. Dr. Mort examined him and found that his in- juries were not of a serious character. It was, how- ever; .deemed advisable to detain him as an in-patient. Oddfellows' Social. A successful social, promoted by the members of the "City of Refuge" Friendly Society of the Loyal Order of Oddfellows (Culcheth) was held in the Risley Presbyterian School the other evening. There was an attendance of about 80. Mr. T. Ball 'officiated as N.C., and Mr. James Henshaw acted as accompanist. Dancing c )mmenc.ed at about eight o'clock and was continued till half-past two next morning. At intervals songs, recitations, &c., were given. Fire at Lymm, _ .

Shortly ater ten o'clock on Tuesday morning the Lymm fire brigade were summoned to Mr. Ben Afoston's house, at Cross-brow, but on their arrival with the neW steam engine the fire had been extin- guished. A cyclist from Wa,rrington saw flames coming from the. bedroom window—some curtains having been set on fire by a child—and he give the alarm at. the Fire Station. This was the first time the new engine had been taken out to a fire,and everything was in readiness had the men had occasion to use. it. Order of Drtilds. With the object of bringing about a uniform system of book-keeping and a uniform sick claim sheet the secretaries of the various lodges of the Warrington Equalized District of the Order of Druids met at the rooms in Corporation-street on Monday evening. G.M. James Ray presided, and was supported by V.G.M. Bibby and others, including:the secretary, Mr. David Houghton. The -fdrins drawn up by the district officials were unanimously approved and will shortly be issued to .the branches. The new sick' claim sheet is considered a great improvement on the Sold one. They AV mid also be supplied with stationery, and would find a staff of. workers to look.after their comforts • generally. All these benefits would be supplied free by the association.

South Lancashire Tramways Bill. ,

The South Lan3ashire Tramways Bill, the preahuhle of which was passed at the last sitting, came, before the Earl of Lauderdule's Committee on Monday, when clauses were submitted to give effect to the Proposals made for the settlement of questions in dispute. Mr. Balfour Browne, K.C., submitted a clause carrying out the committee's decision that the promoters' compul- sory running over the St. Helens tramways should be 1 for passengers only. Mr. Ram, K.C. (for the St. Helens Corporation), thought .the clause was not suffi- ciently specific in providing against the carriage of ,goods.. The Chairman said that what was in the mulcts of the clmmittee was that goods should not be curried but that passengers' luggage to the - extent of 281 b should be allowed. The clause was agreed to. Mr. Balfour Browne said that the question of the 1 Wigan Corporation having running powers in connec- tion with the car shed would be arranged without troubling the Committee. Another clause was agreed i'to providing that all excavated . material used by the promoters should be used within the district of excava- tion. It was further agreed that no lease should be granted by the company without the consent of the local authorities of the district -in which the tram- ways proposed to be leased were situated ; and it is lalso provided that the work at Tyldesley should be carried out within the further extension of fifteen months.—After much discussion the clause of the Bill fixing the stages was agreed to in the following form: 7--That the company might, with the agreement of county councils, fix stages for the regulation of fares in the general principle of a penny a mile, to which was added the proviso that nothing in the clause should prejudice the rights of the Corniration or Leigh as reserved to them by the Act of 1901.—Tt was ordered that the Bill be reported to the House br ,third read- ing. Brunner, Mond and Company. —3O Per Cent

Dividend.

On Wednesday the directors of Messrs Brunner, Mond and Company held a meeting at the Boardroom. Winnington works, Northwich, under the presidency of Sir John T. Brunner, M. P. It was determined to recommend the payment of a dividend of 30 per cent. per annum on the ordinary shares, and 7 per cent. on the preference shares, also to place £125,000 to the re- serve fund and carry forward £37,000. The ordinary dividend for the previous 12 months was 32!;- percent. A Father's Sacrifice. On Wednesday a dro‘vuing accident, under rnosi distressing circumstances, t .ok .piac.t at Ellesmere Port. About dinner time an old man named Henry Fordham, aged 64, who lived at S7,aining Point, wa, rowed accoss the Ship Canal at Bilesnr,•ce Port from the river side to the Ellesmere Port side by his son. After landing him the son pulled the punt back, but just before reaching the shore he fell overblard into deep water. There was a strong tide running, but the old man called to his son to "stick t) the boat" and threw off his coat, and pluckily jumped in to rescue his son. About half way across the canal he threw up his hands and sank. The younger man readied the bank in safety. Personal.

Property Owners and the Corporation.

Several cases affLicting property owners should have been heard at the Borough Police Court on ' Thursday, but when the magistrates took their seats on the Bench Mr. H. Loagton,solicitor, made an ex- planation. He said their Worships fixed that day for the hearing of certain street making objections. Amongst others was a case in which be was interested in respect of Street making in Mill-yard. He was pleased to say that their Worships would not be troubled with his else at any rate ; as the parties had satisfactorily arranged all matters in dispute.— Mr. Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk)said there were three other cases beside the one Mr. Lougton was in, but all the objections had been withdrawn. They Nvere to the provisional apportionment for the forming and paying of certain streets, but he was glad to say that the Court would not be troubled with any of them The Chairman (Mr. John Fairclough): We are not to be troubled with anything?-Ifr. SCHOOL ATTENDANCE OFFICERS' CONFERENCE AT RUNCORN.

The quarterly meeting of the \Varrington and District Branch of the Shool Attendance 0 ficers' National Association was held at the Runcorn Technical institute on Saturday afternoon. Mr. .Tames Pritchard (chair- man of the Runcorn School Board) presided, and there- were also present :—Mr. W. Handley (member of the School Board), Mr. J. W. Lightburn (clerk), Mr. AV. Jones, Warrington (president of-the brlnch) and Messrs R. Garrod and Miller (Birkenhead). W. Rater, Scans- brick, and Bamber (Warrington), Barnes (Leigh), Har- per (Oldham). HenwAll (Salford), Cooper (Farnworth), 'Shard (Winsford), Longworth (Bolton), Astlonry and Davies (Widnes), and T. Bowyer and G. Howard (Run- corn). Letters of apology for non-attendance were read from Messrs. Owen Roberts (Manchester), W. Thomp- son (Crewe). Eli Pickstock (Holmes Chapel), W. H. Proctor (Congleton), Caleb Davies (Northwich), and W.Paskell' (Golborne

• In a felicitous speech Mr Pritchard extended a Fearty, welcome to the members. It was mainly due to the j exertions of the School attendance officers that the 1 children were got to schools. The duties required tact andlikeen judgment. At one time in the early days j parents looked upon them with suspicion but now the !reverse was the ca,se for they hai succeeded in pressing upon the child's parents that it was to their in- terest that the children should attend school. 1 Papers were then read by Mr. Hepwell,of Salford in favour of superannuation and by Mr. Bowyer of Run- corn on "The Education of children dwelling on Canal Boats." The latter paper was a most compre- hensive review of the question and wts the subject of j general eulogium. After Mr. Barnes had read a paper 1 on "The school attendance officer and his work," Votes of thanks closed the meeting the members be- ing subsequently entertained by Mr. Pritchard to tea at 1 Mr. James Lea's restaurant, High-street.

PRESTON ELECTION.

Victory of the Tory Candidate.

Labour Figures Improve.

Kerr (C) Hodge (Lab.)

Conservative Majority

The above is the result of the polling at Preston, as declared shortly after ten o'clock on Thursday night, ,to a crowd of some 25,000 people who had congregated at the Town Hall. The figures were received with enthusi- asm by the Conservatives, for there had been some anxiety as to the result.

IMPORTANT TO ALL.

Every person's future is, to a certain extent, in his own band. Many a future which might have been brilli- ant has been darkened by lack of the spirit of self-help. Many a home, which might have been gladdened by life and health, and comfort, has been saddened by poverty, illness, and death, in many cases wholly preventible. The spring and summer months are, from a sanitary point of view, among the most important of the year. Due care may now mean a healthy and happy summer and autumn, and robust constitution to meet the trials of next winter. Neglect may now mean a joyless summer, a trying autumn, and a broken constitution to succumb under the attacks of any exceptional trial. There are, indeed, few that do not feel the system needs recruiting after the trying winter they have passed through. With some it is" only a little cough," with others, "a tendency to bronchitis," with others "not trite up to the mark," " a sort of all-over sinking feeling," "to weak to do anything about the house," "these recurring headaches,"" no appetite for my food," etc. These and a dozen others are common expressions, which all point to the urgent need of some good tonic. What change of air can do for you in a few months time—if you are no worse than you are now—a course of some good tonic mixture, a reliable medicine of established reputation aryl of proved virtue will do now. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES,

• OBSTRUCTING THE TRANIW TRAFFIC. At the Warrington Police Court on Friday, Thomas Hudson, carrier, Culcheth, was summoned for impeding traffic on the Corporation Tramways on April 27th.— Mr. S. Wilson, deputy town clerk, prosecuted and said the 5 15 p.m. car from Latchford was proceeding along Knutsford-road and when opposite Mersey View it overtook the defendant who was in charge of a landau. He was driving on the track and the driver sounded the gong. Defendant took no notice and the driver repeat- .edly sounded the gong whereupon defendant simply shook his whip. He continued to drive in front of the .car until he reached Mersey-street when he turned off the track.—Evidence having been given in support of this statement, defendant stated that he kept to his own side but there were two lurries being driven in front of him. Despite this, he was driving at a rate of six or seven miles an hour.—Thomas Hough, Glazebury supported defendant's version.—The Chairman (Mr. H. Roberts) said some of the magistrates thought they ought to fine defendant 20s. ancosts as it seemed to be a deliberate case of obstruction, but the majority were in favour of a penalty of 10s. and costs. Future offenders would be more severely dealt.with. PAUPER'S SINGULAR CONDUCT—How NEW CLOTHES

ARE OBTAINED. At the Warrington Police Court, on Saturday, before Messrs. Birchall (in the chair) R. S. Faiirclough, J. W. Hayward, Henry Thornton, and Dr. Fox, Richard James Braide acasual inmate of the Warrington Union Work- house, was charged with refusing to perform the task allotted to him.—John Constable, porter at the Work- house, said that the prisoner was given some cleaning to do that morning, but refused to perform the task— Prisoner complained of the food he received. He was only allowed bread and water.—The Chairman: We are not supposed to find champagne and oystersand that sort of thing. (Laughter). You will have to go to gaol for 14 days, and you will have to work there— George Savage, another inmate of the workhouse was 'charged with destroying his clothing on Friday.—The porter stated that the prisoner was admitted to the ward on Thursday night, but he refused to do any work, and tore all his clothing into rags.—The Chief Con- stable : It (theclothing) looks as if he had fallen from a motor-car. (Laughter.)—The Porter said the man refused to wear the boots and had to be taken to the station without any. He said the boots were not good enough for him.—The Chairman: Perhaps he wanted patent leather boots. (Laughter).—The Chief Con- stable said that when paupers wished to obtain fresh clothing they did what was called "a tear up," so that the ratepayers could supply them with better clothing.— Theßench committed Savage to goal for two months.

Minor Cases.

Warrington Borough Police Court.

MONDAY.—Before Mr. F. Monks (presiding), Alderman Smethurst, and Messrs. J. Geddes, T. Birchall, J. W. Hayward, S. Wright and H. Thornton. A BAD RECORD.—Mary Rosser was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Bank-street, at 9.15 on Satur- day, night. She pleaded not uilty.—Police-constable Gibbs proved the case, and said the woman was using most filthy language, and refused to desist. He had to get the assistance first of a postman, and afterwards of Police-constable Taylor to get her to the police station. —The Chief Constable said the prisoner had been fined ss. and costs on March 26, April 26, and May 4, but she had not paid the fines, and she had gone to goal on each occasion.—The Bench decided to send prisoner to gaol for one month. DISORDERLY CosnucT.William Donoghue, labourer, Hitydock-street„ and Mary Jane Burke, Dutton-street, were fined ss. each for making unseemly noises in the streets.—John Mullin, a fofgeman, living, in Dial-street, was bound over to keep the peace for disorderly con- duct in Dial-street.—This was defendant's 30th appstif- ance for various offences.

TUESDAX.—Before Dr. Fos (presiding), Alderman Hutchinson and Mr. J. R. Young. AN IMPUDENT TEIIEF.—John McDonald, a labourer, last from Mancheste, was charged on remand with using obscene language in Howley-lane on the night of May 4, and also with stealing on the same date, a tin of corned beef, of the value of 3s„ from the shop of Messrs. - Povey and Duckworth, provision dealers, Butternaarket-street.—The Chief Constable stated that on the Monday in question the prisoner was going from shop to shop offering a tin of corned beef for sale at a ridiculously 'lox price, and when the shopkeeper's refused to buy it he made use of the most vile and abusive language. He was pointed out to Police- Constable Sweeney, who went after him. Prisoner bolted, but the officer caught him and brought him to the Police Station. As prisoner was unable to give a satisfactory account of the beef, he was remanded so as to enable the police to find an owner for it. It was ascer- tained that the beef had been stolen from Messrs.Povey and Duckworth's shop. The prisoner had evidently gone into the shop on some e use and baken the tin when unobserved. Prisoner saidhe was going to Chester with three or four companions, and he thought the beef must have been purchased by one of them.—He was sent to gaol for a month.

Reports.

SATISFACTORY FINANCIAL POSITION.

Interesting details of the operations of the Warring- ton Rugby Football Club during the past season are furnished in the annual reports of the Secretary and Treawrer which have just been issued and will be submitted for consideration at the annual meeting on Tuesday evening next. The Secre- tary (Mr. H. Ashton) reports as follows ; "In presenting my annual report I.have great pleasure in stating thatthe Ist team. have played 35 matches and of these 14 were won, 14 lost, and 7 drawn. The club attained the eighth position in the Northern League Competition, scoring 35 points out of a possible 70.

===== 0002601_19030515_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SEASON'S MATCHES.

Opening the season at Swinton, a defeat by 12 points to nil was not an inspiring beginning. Success, how- ever, followed, the team beating Brighouse, St. Helens, and Batley,also having much the best of a draw with Runcorn. Salford were very fortunate to take 2 points. Halifax away was a very fine performance, we having a decided advantage on the day's play. Leigh proved tough opponents,. a very exciting game resulting,ina win by 11 points to 9. A defeat at Hull. then a success by a narrow margin against, Oldham. The following 3 matches were away and only 1 point was gained— at Broughton. A win at Widnes preceded Brad- ford, who scored 10 points tto nil at War- rington. Away matches with Leigh and Hudders- field ended disastrously, and brought about the end of the year. An advantage on the game with Swinton— each team getting one point—opened the New Year. after successive defeats by Brighouse and Runcorn each away, we saw Batley visiting our ground to play the return, and a welcome win after a stubborn contest brought two points. Salford next paid their return visit and bagged two points again. Another misfortune at St. ,Helens in the Cup-tie proved the inconsistency of the team. Fortune hereafter seemed to be with us, a series of wins over St. Helens and Hull, another draw with Broughton Ringers, then two points at the expense of Hull Kingston Rovers, being recorded. A loss at Oldham; then Hunslet met witlia most decisive defeat, the combination of our backs being really splendid, and resulting in a score of 16 points to seven. ‘Vigan were decide* lucky to nuke a draw with us on their own ground. Good Friday brought Halifax here, and with it a grand game, a record gate, also a popular victory. Beating Widnes next day, hopes were entertained of beating Wigan Z on the Monday, but unfortunately the team played very indifferently and lost by a dropped goal to nil. Bradford was the last away match, honours being easy after a fine exposition of the game, each side scoring 11 points. With the last fixture of the season came a victory over Huddersfield by 2 tries to one try. The "A" team have had a very successful season. Out of 31 matches played 20 were won, 8 lost and 3 drawn, the team occupying 3rd position in the Lancashire Combination.

===== 0002601_19030515_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW PLAYERS.

Altogether 23 new players were signed on during the season, 13 of the most promisinr, who had taken part in the Works Competition and 10 promising other sources.

FINES, ETC.

On one occasion only has the Club suffered the in- dignity of being reported and fined for late start, and that occurred in the Cup-tie at St. Helens.

RECORDS.

The largest gate ever taken by the Club was with Halifax, on Good Friday, £257 being recorded at the gates. More points than ever previously scored in any competition, viz., 35. Seven County players have been regularplaying members with our team this season.

COUNTY MATCHES.

Harris and Boardman had the honour of selection and pliyed for the Champion County. Fish played a dis- tinguished part in the majority of the County fixtures and Dickenson was chosen to play in the trial games, as well as assisting against Cumberland.

INJURED PLAYERS. Starting the season, the Captain was absent froA.,the Ist team for several weeks, then Fish was incapacitated and later a damaged hand kept him out of the team for some time. Dickenson's suspension wasanother serious loss, then an attack of quinsey prostrated him for a long period. Isherwood again about the same period was un tble to do himself justice owing to an injured leg. Taylor, Morrison, with others at various periods laave been more or less damaged.

===== 0002601_19030515_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
FIXTURES.

A new departure was adopted in the matter of arrang- ing for fixtures -last season. Previously the secretaries attended a meeting convened for that purpose and fixed up among themselves; then a system was introduced of the drawing for the privilege of being the first club to arrange. Last season these methods were discar- ded, and an idea was tried on the lines of the Association football. By this system even our list came out fairly good, but owing to the many fixture lists gett- ing mixed, a sub-committee appointed for that purpose revised every Club's fixtures, with the result that two good home matches--Salford and Hull Kingston—were reversed. This was a great misfortune undoubtedly, forcing us to play 12 matches away to 5 at home for the first half of the season.

===== 0002601_19030515_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
RETROSPECT.

During the close seasonall the players were exhorted by your Committee to go in for sprint running as much as possible, and with that object in view, and to encour- age them pumps were provided by the club, besides every facility being afforded in the matter of dressing room and baths. Many of the players availed them- selves of the offer, benefiting greatly in their speed and general fitness. With the:advent of the season, practice matches were held twice each week, trials to the most promising juniors being afforded. The form displayed augured well for a successful season, these hopes being verified for the first few weeks. Injuries combined with indifferent forward play, and so many away matches, were the principal causes of failure during the middle portion of the season. This period was a very anxious time for your Committee. Their energy,however, did not abate, efforts to secure good class players being made, but good offers failed to procure men whose reputation fitted them for a position in our first team. Half-backs have been a debatable question through the season, in fact almost every Club in the Leaguehas been very anxious and willing to pay a big price for a good fast half. Personally, ffeel that our forward division has been the weakest point for several seasons .past. Football generally did not reach the standard anticip- ated with the introduction of the kick-in-touch rule. The come-up system with the forwards also was a very unsatisfactory feature throughout the season. Certainly something will have to be done to obviate this obnoxi- ou.s practice. Perhaps the limitation of players to 12 or 13 aside, if adopted, may bring about this most desirable change. The players have throughout the season expressed their satisfaction with the handsome treatment shown to them by your committee. My best thanks are due to Mr. T. Pemberton for his invaluable assistance and advice, and to the committee individually and collectively for their loyalty and support." Detailed records of the scoring in each match are submitted, together with the Northern League and West Lancashire Cup Competition tables. TREASURER'S REPORT, _

The .Treasurer (Mr. T. Pemberton) presents the following report :—"I have the pleaQure to submit to you the balance sheet for the season just ended. There has been a slight falling off in the membership. Gate money produced £1,69?65. Bd. afterpayments to wirious junior clubs, and £lO to the Wigan football club to induce them to play on Easter Monday the match which was postponed through frost. It is pleasing to note that the gross gate for "A" team matches produced £7l lls, more than last season. There was a less attendance at the first team matches, owing to the un- ftivourable weather, until Easter. the gates for these matches bringing in over t 456, which together with the exceptional profit made at the sports accounts for the large balance in hand. The bar is proving to be a success, the profit showing a gradual increase. The committee decided to change their old scheme of insurance and insure with the British Dominion Insurance Co. Ltd., and this has proved beneficial. Latchford Rangers have been pro- vided with a field and our ground has been placed at their disposal on three occasions. In the payments the expenses of maintaining the ground have been rather heavy, this being the year for painting, and the storm a few weeks ago caused a lot of damage to the fencing. Rates and taxes and refreshments are larger. The other items of expenditure compare xery favourably with past seasons. The Works Competition resulted in a profit of £69 18s. and this amount has been devoted to charities after providing for seven players who re- ceived injuries. Before concluding I should like to call attention to the fact that the lease of the ground expires in September 1908, and to impress upon you the necessity ef preparing, in the event of our having to remove to a new ground."

THE ACCOUNTS.

The statement of accounts shows that the receipts for the year including a cash balance of £232 13s. 6d. brought forward from last year, were £2,342 14s. 6d. The entrance fees and subscriptions amounted to £199 9s. 6d. and the profit on the sports was £ll3 78. 11d. A REAL BAD BACK.

There are good backs and bad backs. Some backs are bad. Mr. Charles Braithwaite, of Skelsmergh, Kendal, describes his as areal bad back. Someitimes he went for days half doubled up. At times he had a pain so sharp right through his back it seemed to knock him down, and he could not stir for a quarterof an hour. and dared not try. In bed, he could not turn over without suffer- ing excruciating pains. He is entirely better now and !stronger every way, Since taking Dodd's Kidney Pills, pain in the back has been a thing of the past. "I have not lost any work since," he says, "and I have been wet through once or twice nearly every week." Dodds Kidney Pills cure backache because they cure the kidneys. Backache is'nt the fault of the back, its the fault of the kidneys, and real bad backs are made good and become strong ones when the kidneys are well That is what Dodd's Kidney Pills do—make well kidneys.

Runcorn Petty Sessions.

MONDAY.—Before Messrs. F. J. Norman (in the chair),

and E. Imison.

TEMPORARY ATJTHORITY,—Joseph Dutton labourer, was granted temporary authority to sell at the "Village Inn, a beerhouse in Halton lately kept by Thomas Wilkinson.

A NEGLECTFUL MAN.—John Dale, hawker, was summoned by Superintendent Ennion on behalf of the Inspector of the Industrial School, London, for the sum of £2 2s. 6d. being arrears due on the 31st March for the maintenance of his son, James, at present in the Industrial School at Chaster.—The evidence showed that the defendant had not made a single payment under the order and was in constant work either labouring or hawking.—The magistrates ordered the arrears to be paid within the month one half within fourteen days. . A YOUTHFUL OFFENDER.—James Rose, Gilbert street, was summoned for playing pitch and toss on the 3rd May.—The evidence of P. C. Gregory showed that the defendant with other boys' was playing pitch and toss in Waterloo Archway. Defendant ran away when the policeman made a dash for hini. Fined ss. including costs or seven days. OBSTRUCTION.—Teresa Yarwood, King street, was summoned for obstructing the footpath in High street on the 30th March.—P. C. Edwards proved the case. Fined ss. Er in default 7 days. AN UNRITLY YOUNG WOMAN.—Mary Ellen Dykes, Speakman-street, was summoned for throwing stones in Devonshire-square on the Ist May to the danger of passers-by.—P.C. Gregory and Edwards proved the stone throwing, also obscene language and unruly conduct in the street.—Remanded for a fortnight. OBSCENE tANGUAGE.—Martha Hill, Mill-brow, was lined ss. for using obscene language on the 2nd May, on the evidence of P.C. Edwards.—There were forty-sir previous convictions, the last being three years ago.— Martha Woods, Cooper-street, was fined ss. for using obscene language on the evidence of P.C. Gregory.

DRUNK THROUGH HIS WIFE'S DEATH.--Thomas O'Connor was summoned for being drunk and disorderly on Saturday.—The evidence showed that the prisoner came to the police station and would not go away. Prisoner stated that he had only buried his wife on the Saturday and had a drop of drink with another man, after the funeral inhis home. The "other man " struck him and smashed the furniture. He came to ,the police station to report the matter. He had never been in a police court in his life before.—Prisoner was discharged w th a caution.

HORSEMAN v. TRAMC4R. Grak3penhall Publican Fined for Obstruction. NOTICE OF APPEAL GIVEN.

A case which raised a number of interesting points occupied the attention of Mr. F. Monks and other magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Monday John Thomas, licensee of the Church House Inn, Grap- penhall, and a well known horse trainer being summoned for wilfully impeding traffic on the Corporation tram- ways in Knutsford-road on April 28th.—Mr. Stanley Wilson, deputy town clerk, prosecuted, and the defendant, who denied the offence, was represented by Mr. A. Browne.

Mr. Wilson said the defendant had for some time been in the habit of bringing young horses along the Knuts- ford-road so as to get them used to the trains. Upon the 10.40 car from Rylands-stieet arriving at. Thelwall- lane, on the morning in question, the driver noticed the defendant, who was leading a horse, put up his hand and at the same time deliberately pull the animal directly in front of the car. The car was twenty yards off, but the driver 'applied the brake and brought the tram to a standstill within a yard of the defendant. Mr. Thomas began to argue with the driver, who re- peatedly asked him to get out of the way. Defendant absolutely refused, and said the driver ought to stop. The car was held up for three minutes, and the driver, had to start it again before the defendant would get out of the way. Defendant threatened to hold the car up on the return journey, and he did so near the Cheshire Cheese for about two minutes. Defendant seemed to be under the impression that he could stop a car whenever he liked. He ventured to say that would not beallowed for one moment. It was a question of whether the cars were to provide an efficient service or to be interfered with by anyone.

Mr. ilrorine: Or to be smashed to atoms by a young horse.

Mr. Wilson said he would prove that the horse was not restive and there was no risk. The case was not brought with the intention of obtaining a heavy penalty, but to show defendant that he was not allowed to interfere with the cars.

Samuel Smith, driver of the car, gave evidenoe and denied that the horse was restive. Defendant had not told him on a previous occasion that he ought to stop when he saw a young horse and that drivers Ellis and Warburton always did so. From time to time drivers had assisted the defendant and other gentlemen who had charge of young horses. William Baxendale, conductor, corroborated. Adam Taylor, green grocer, 137, PaAlgate-lane, was called and said he was of the opinion that the obstruc- tion was deliberate. The horse did not appear to be at all restive.

Police Sergeant Evans said he was on duty near the Railway Inn on the day in question. He saw Thomas who wasleadiag a horse in the centre of the road, put up his hand when the car came along. The car then slowed down and stopped, and he &WI what appeared to be an argument going on between the driver and Thomas After a delay of a couple of minutes or so, he saw Thomas coming towards him and when he got to the Railway Inn witness asked what was the matter. He said, "He won't stop, but I will make him. I have got a very valuable horse, but I will make him stop." Witness told him he would be liable to be summoned if he did, bat he replied : " I don't care, I will make him stop again." He then went down the road, and when by the Cheshire Cheese on the return journey of the car, Thomas turned his horse round, and brought it in the front of the car. The driver stopped for two or three minutes, and then both proceeded on their way.—ByMr.Browne: Witness was 80 or 100 yards away when defendant stopped the car. For the defence. Mr. Browne sAid this was a most important matter. Mr. Thomas was a horse trainer of

repute, and had some very valuable horses to train. He submitted that it was to the advantage of the Electric- ity Committee and of the whole of the community that horses should be trained to meet the cars. He pointed out that drivers Ellis and Warburton always stopped for the defendant, as tbeylwere formerly employed on the 'bus service and had a knowledge of horses, whereas Smithihad not. There was no obstruction except for a few seconds. The horse in question was worth 100 guineas, and defendant had brought it out to get it accustomed to the cars. What more laudable object could there be? On hearing the car coming the horse pricked up its ears and commenced to plunge, and defendant put tip his inuid for the driver to stop. Mr. Lunt's gardener also saw the danger and shouted to the car driver, who, however' did not stop until within a yard of the defendant. The horse was rearing and plunging and Mr. Thomas could not control it. After the car had been stopped a few seconds the horse became quiet. The driver put the car on full speed, which caused the horse to agaia plunge right against Mr. Lunt's gate endanger- ing itself and Mr. Thomas. Was there any obstruction in that? He submitted that before they could convict there would have to be certain things done. One had not been done, whioh be would not mention then. Could they call it wilful obstruction, if a man was drivinga young horse, and could not get it out of the way? The Chairman called Mr. Browne's attention to the fact that he had not cross-examined Sergeant Evans. Mr. Browse: The least said about that the better. I shall not say why. There were three persons present when the report was given to the sergeant. The Chief Constable said that in all fairness to the officer they ought to be called. Was it fair to allege something against the truth of the sergeant's evidence? Mr. Browne: I was not alleging that. The Chief Constable: It is my duty to protect my men.

Mr. Browne: Sergeant Evans was 120 yards away from where the alleged obstruction took place. It would be the act of an insane man to deliberately put a valuable horse in front of the car. The Chairman: You draw your own inferences. Walter Crompton, passenger on the car in question, Mid the car was stopped twenty or thirty seconds. He did not think there was any necessity for a charge of obstruction. Defendant said the animal was out for the first time to meet the trams. The driver of the car did not take any notice when he (defendant) put up his hand, and the horse jumped forward and pulled witness in front of the car. He did not impede the tram for two minutes; neither did he tell Sergeant 'Evans that he would ob- struct the oar again. Mr. Patterson, cycle agent, said he rode behind the oar on his bicycle. The car was not stopped two minutes. He did not think the case ought to have been brought to Court. Mr. Wilson: Did you see the car stopped at all? Witness: No. Mr. Wilson: Then how do you know how long it was stopped? Witness: Well, how long wduld it take me to get to my house from the Black Bear Bridge? Mr. Wilson: That depends where you called at. (Laughter.) Mr. Browne (warmly) : He is not In the habit of calling. (Moro laughter.) Don't make insinuations. Mr. Wilson: He might have had an important cus- tomer to call on.

William Green, gardener in the employ of Mr. Lunt, Latchford, also gave evidence,and said the car was only stopped about half a minute.

After consulting in private, the Bench fined defendant 20s. including costs, and Mr. Browne gave notioe of appeal to Quarter Sessions.
