Won upon a time an American taunted an Englishman: "How can you endure to be taxed to support your idle nobility?" exclaimed the American warmly. Then the American paid £3 a ton for his coal in order that the directors of the company might procure titled men for sons-in-law. This fable teaches that there are almost as many ways of paying taxes as of dodging the same. A CONSEQUENTIAL-LOOKING little man enterep the commercial-room of a hotel and gave a vigorous pull at the bell. As no one answered, he rang again more loudly than before. The maid-servant then came bustling in, and the following colloquy took place: Servant: "Who rang that belt V Little Man (making the most of his height): "I did." Servant (scornfully): "And who lifted you up to it?" A VERY calculating young man has kept for two year an accurate account of every penny he has spent in theatre tickets, carriages, flowers and gloves in connection with each lady he has invited out, inclusive of chaperons. On the credit side of that day book of pleasure is recorded his estimates of the satisfaction the lady gave him in return for the money expended. ThiG prudent man just turns to his account-book before inviting, and by comparing the record sees from which charmer he gets most for his money I "So you refuse to give me the money?" said the prodigal son. "Not another penny do you get,' replied the stern parent. "Then here goes," said the youth, as he seized a silver. mounted pistol from his father's desk. " Un- happy boy?" cried the old man, "would you take your life?" "No," replied the son, "I'm going to lend this to my 'uncle.'" "I THOUGHT you used to say that you wouldn't care to be tied to Miss Wheeler, if she were the last girl in the world; but I see that you're paying a good deal of attention to her lately," said Brown to his friend Green, "Yes," said Green," my opinion of her has undergone a com- plete change. I used to think that she would not make a hood wife for a poor man, because she seemed to be lazy." "And now?" asked Brown. "Why, look at her bicycle! There isn't another girl in town who keeps her wheel in such fine condition."

A TEACHItiI, wishing to impress upon the minds of her pupils the full nature of faith, took them one day to the river-side, and, seeing a boat in the middle of the stream, said: "Now, my little dears, if I were to tell you that there was a leg of mutton in than boat, would you believe me!" "Yes." "Well, then," she said, "that is faith." Seme time after the same children were going through an examination, when the question was asked, "What is faith?" and all the class as with one voice, shouted out: "A leg of mutteii in a boat."

Hz stopped at the corner, gazed into the sky, scratched his chin, and pulled his whiskers. Then he fumbled is his pocket, wrinkled his brow, pursed up his lips, and blew his nose in a manner more deliberate than the collector of z bad debt. Anon he opened his mouth, tugged at his lower lip, muttered to himself, and fixed his eyes on the chimneptope in a vacant, stony stare, that soon collected a crowd, which blockaded the pavement. He was a married man trying to think what it was his wife had charged him to be sure and net.

Militia Training.

About 200 of the 4th Battalion King's Liverpool Regiment left Bank Quay Station, on Monday after- noon for the annual training at Altear. The men were headed by the band, and were in *command of Captain Jenkins, with Lieutenant Batten as adjutant. They will be away about a mouth.

Bigamy at Warrington. In die Divorce Court, on Monday, Mr. Justice Nandi had before him the case of Bromley v. Brom- ley. This was a suit of Mrs. Charlotte Bromley, re" siding at Newport (Monmouthshire), for a divorce by reason of the desertion, bigamy, and adultery of her husband, William Bromley, with Ruth M'Lean Blenkinsopp, at Warrington. There was no defence. It was stated that the petitioner and her husband were married on May 20, 1891, at Newport. and had lived at Redland-street. In April, 1900, the respondent deserted his wife. Subsequently he was arrested and tried for bigamy at Liverpool and sentenced to four years' penal servitude. Ruth M' Lean BlenkinsoPP said that she lived at Warrington, and in 1900 she made the acquaintance of the respondent, and on the 29th-December, 1900, she went through the ceremoni of marriage with him. In February, 1902,he left. her, and she had since had a child. Mr. Richard Sell, Lather of the petitioner, a glass-bottle maker, at port, gave gave evidence in support of the case. A decree nisi was granted. Apprehended at Padgate. _ _ _ -

On Monday, at Eceles„Tohn Roberts and William Owen were brought up on remand charged with steal- ing two watches and other jewellery, from a house lu Higher Croft, Barton, on Friday afternoon. After the theft the defendants made for Warrington, and thl were apprehended the same evening at Padgate Police-constable Smith, who went in pursuit on hi° bicycle. The articles stolen were found in their possession. Mr. Superintendent Keys aziked for 3, further remand, as the defendants refused to give ail account of themselves, and they are to be photograph- ed.—The application was granted.

LICENSED VICTUALLERS' BANQUET. Speeches by Colonel Hall Walker, M.P., Mr. R, Pierpoint. M.P., and Mr. A. H. Crosfield.

The Confiscation of Licences.

The members of the Warrington and District Licensed Victuallers' Association, which was founded in 1848 with the object of protecting the trade interests of those connected with it and establishing a fund for the relief of distressed members, and widows and children of deceased members, assembled at the Lion Hotel for their second annual banquet in aid of. the Benevolent Fund of the Association. Excel- lent arrangements had been made by the Banquet Committee with Councillor John Evans as presi- dent and Mr. T. Walsh as seeretary, and the function passed off with complete success. A sumptuous repast was served, Mrs. Gilbert catering in her best style. Beautiful flowers had been utilized for table decoration and the room presented a very bright and cheerful appearance. Colonel W. Hall Walker M.P. presided and the large attendance included Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., Mr. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., Mr. A. H. Crosfield, Mr. Roger C. Parr, Mr. James Monro, Mr. J. Fulton Smith, Mr. W. B. Roberts, Mr. R. W. ltowson, Councillor John Evans (President of the Association), Mr. Joseph Shillcock (vice-president), Mr. R. Shacklady (treasurer), Mr. T. Walsh (secretary), Councillor Isaac Turner (representing the National Defence League), of Liverpool; Mr. A. Browne, Alderman Francom.b, Rev. Father Wilson, Mr. Reginald Mortimer (representing the Northern Brewers' Association), Mr. John Wrench (president of the local branch of the Wine and Beersellers' Association), Mr. T. Walsh (secretary), Alderman Bolton, Councillor Baird, Mr. J. Clarke, Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, and others. Deputations were present from Manchester, Liverpool, Wigan' Oldham, Rochdale, and Bolton. After dinner the usual toast list was gone through, Mr. R. Shacklady acting as toast master. THE LOYAL TOASTS.

The CHAIRMAN proposed the health of the King, and said that on the occasion of the Coronation no section of community put their hands deeper into their pockets to show their loyalty than the licensed victuallers of Warrington. Referring to the King's continental tour he said it might foretell European peace for years to come.

The toast was heartily honoured "God save the King" being sung. "Queen AlGxandra, the Prince and Princess of Wales, and the rest of the Royal family" was proposed by the Chairman and duly honoured.

ARMY, NAVY AND AUXILIARY FORCES. In proposing this toast Mr. ARTHUR BROWNE re- ferred to the speeches of Sir John Fisher and Mr. Broderick at the Royal Academy banquet. He thought that if Sir John Fisher was able to say that the navy was safe, all of them might consider that it was safe in the able hands that it was placed mat the present time. The President of the Academy expressed a wish that the army and navy might be more joined together in their work, and he (Mr. Browne) thought they would all agree that the more co-operation and co-ordination there was between the two forces, the greater benefit would accrue to the country. Alluding to Mr. Broderick's remarks with reference to Lord Kitchener, Mr. Browne said it appeared that the Government had some idea of trouble on the Indian frontier, but he sincerely hoped this would not be the case.

The toast was enthusiastically honoured. In reply, Mr. REGINALD MORTIMER said that during the past few years all branches of the service had been subjected to vigorous, and he thought he might justly say in some cases ungenerous criticism. Once again, however, the cause of justice and right had triumphed, but with the generosity which had ever distinguished the people of our race, we had held out the right hand of fellowship to those with whom we had recently been at war and had offered to them the blessings of citizen- ship of this great empire. He thought we had taught the people of the world that to quarrel with England did not mean to fight with the inhabitants of these islands alone but the quarrel must be with all those, wherever they might dwell, who owned Edward VII as King. Mr. Mortimer referred to the need for efficiency in all branches of the service and said that in order to secure it means must be provided. He thought, that of all the great contributories to the country's ex- penditure no war minister could appeal with confidence to anyone more than to the members of that trade to whom it was his privilege to speak that night. (Applause). MR. W. B. ROBERTS AND 4 THE COMPENSATION

QUESTION.

In proposing "The Houses of Parliament," Mr. W. B. ROBERTS said the most important Bill before Parlia- ment as far as they were concerned was the question of compensation for licences. Tho Dill introduced by Mr. Butcher was carried by a substantial majority of two to one, namely, 266 to 134 on the second reading, and that was highly satisfactory, inasmuch as it expressed the strong public feeling upon the question of compensa- tion as a principle. But in that Bill he was afraid there were several conditions that they could not accept. He did not for one moment anticipate that the trade with satisfaction or even justice could accept the condition that compensation was to come out of the pockets of brewers and licensed victuallers. The trade for some number of years had been burdened with taxation for every benefit but its own, and as far back as 1890, Mr. Goschen raised the duty on beer 3d. per barrel, whieh was increased in 1894 by .Sir William Harcourt 6d. per barrel, and in 1900 Sir Michael Hicks-Beach increased that duty by ls. as a war tax, making is. 9d. per barrel. That duty was now in operation. As regarded spirits,the duty had been increased from the period 1890 by is. per gallon-6d. in 1890 by Mr. Goschen, and 6d. in 1894 by Sir William Harcourt. In 1895, Sir William Harcourt reduced it to 10s. 6d., and in 1900, as a special war tax, the 6d. was re-imposed by the present Government—a total on beer aid spirits of 2s. 6d. since 1890.- The in- crease of that taxation, equal to £440,000 per annum on a capital practically of six millions since 1890, was for specific objects, the first to raise a fund for com- pensation for licences. but converted to technical edu- cation, and the second as a war tax, which had no • happily terminated. They had done in the past, and were now doing,their share of contribution to the National Exchequer,but with all that it was still proposed that another burden of increased taxation should be placed on their shoulders by contributing a sum not exceeding 6s. Bd. per cent. on the ascertained interest of the owner in the licence per annum, and the like amount by every licensed person on the ascertained interest of the licensed person in the licence, to form a fund for Compensation. That, he felt sure. the trade would never accept. What i was the alternative? He suggested that a portion of the money now paid as duty on beer and spirits be apportioned with a similar amount from the local rates to form a fund for the compensation for licences, so that the obligation should fall in some measure on the locality that:desired the reduction of licences. (Hear, hear.) It was only right that they, as a trade, should guard themselves against the reckless abolition of licences, which would only too readily occur, unless the cost and responsibility was shared by those who were ever ready to expressitheir injustice and animosity to their business. They must guard it with every jealousy, and it behoved them to demand of all members of Parliament their full and unequivocal support. Any other positi9n must be met with stern oppo- sition. It was not a matter of politics. It had too long been that, but they must look to the preservation of their living and their families. He regarded the means of ascertaining the value of a licence as being cumbersome in its operation. The Bill in Parliament placed the onus on the owner and licensee to declare theirrespective interests in their proportion which, he was sure, would end in continual dissatisfac- tion and litigation. He had suggested through the columns, of the "Brewers' Journal" that the basis of compensation should be five years' gross receipts. He took it gross receipts and establishment charges varied with the position of a house, and he had apportioned that compensation into three parts—the owner 30 per cent., tbe brewer 50 per cent., and the licensee 20 per cent. The brewer might only be the lessee, and he lost his trade, while the owner retained hisproperty convert- able into other uses. All parties were entitled to com- pensation, and none more than the licensed victualler. On the deternination of the justices to abolish a licence he would for the purpose of valuation appoint the receiver of bankruptcy to assess the proportion, that authority being in full possession of the machinery to deal with matters of income. It was only by a deter- mined, combinedand watchful position that they could obtain these things, and they must demand the support of the members that they returned to Parliament to maintain the acknowledgment of a vested interest,which in all matters of taxation was so regarded by the law. (Applause.)

MR. PIERPOINT'S REPLY.

A BOGUS FOOTBALLER'S VISIT TO LONDON AMUSING STORY.

Mr. ROBERT PIERPOENT, M.P., responded and in re- ferring to Mr. Butcher's Compensation Bill said he was one of the members who formed the large majority by which the second reading was carried. He voted for that Bill, being convinced that compensation was the just right of the licensed victuallers on the grounds that if for the benefit or the supposed benefit of the State

===== 0002601_19030515_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Friday, May 15, 1903.

the property was taken away from a man, it was only right and just that compensation should be givento the man thus deprived. He was not going to compare their trade with a trade long ago passed away, but as an example—of course it was a hackneyed example but it was certainly a good one—he would mention that when some seven years ago slavery was abolished it was considered right—and he thought perfectly justly—that those who had their slaves taken away from them, slavery being then a legal institution, should be compensated for the property that . was taken away from them. Also when purchase in the army was done away. with, compensation was given to the holders of commissions who had bought those commissions as was formerly the custom. Upon the question of compensation there was no doubt in the public mind a large amount of con- fusion. As an example he would tell them that amongst several letters he had received about the Bill he received one which pressed him to vote against the Butchers' Compensation Bill." (Laughter.) Mr, Pierpoint pro- ceeded to refer to the toast, and speaking of the House of Lords said he was no infrequent visitor to hear the debates there, and he need not say that their debates differed very much from those in the House of Commons. In the House of Lords there was not that seeking afLir self advertisement which was rather pre- valent in the House of Commons. There a gentleman represented a constituency from which he might be thrown in the course of a few years, and consequently he strove in the House of Commons to show his cow stituency in the country what a great man he was. In the House of Lords the noble peers held their seats for life without having to appeal to constituencies, and he confessed that in many ways the peers were very much more business-like than the Commons. As to the House of Commons he need scarcely tell them that he was very proud indeed of sitting for them and his native town in the Commons of England. The longer he was there—and it was nearly eleven years I now since he first went there—the more he liked it, and the more he got used to it, and he hoped to sit in that House notwithstanding the very worthy and proper efforts of his excellent friend—(laughter and applause) —for a good many years to come. (Applause). Besides having to vote, now and then to speak and to attend and be ready, the House of Commons was a place which in its way taught one to keep one's eyes open. A very decent looking young man called upon him in London some time ago and said he was employed at Messrs. Walkers' in Warrington. He added that he played left back or something like that—(laughter)—in the War- rington football team and had come to London to look for some players for the Warrington team. "Of course" I continued Mr. Pierpoint "I ought to have known that ! a Lancashire team would not want to go to London for its players—(laughter)—but such was my innocence that I did not know." 'Well' said the man with an air of deference and contrition. I have been very unTortunate. I drank too much and ' was robbed. I have'nt gotenongh money even to pay , for a telegram. I have plenty of money locked up at home—(laughter)—and I want to get back to Warring- ton.' I said `Mr. I daresay it will surprise you to ; . hear that your case is not a singular one. Will you kindly give me the name of some responsible person in Warrington to whom I may telegraph and when I-have had a reply I will tell you what I will do?' Oh yes' he Isaid',Of course in my place at Messrs. Walkers' I ' an brought largely into connection with the publicans in Warrington. I will give you the name of Mr. Brown, " Royal Oak, High-street." Of course" again con- fessed Mr. Pierpoint "I ought to have known where the Royal Oak was but I could not be expected at ten o'clock in the morning in London to know where the Royal Oak in Warrington was, so I innocently took down the name of Brown, Royal Oak, High-street. Then he gave me the name of somebody else. I telegraphed to Brown, Royal Oak, High-street, Warrington, and got a note from the telegraph office in the afternoon in- forming me 'Not'at that address. Tried Brown, Seven Stars, says it is not for him.' (Great laughter.) I need scarcely say my friend had left me before the telegrams occured and although he had willingly con- sented to call again, at three in the afternoon, I never saw anything more of him. I have reason to believe that my honourable friend (Colonel Walker) had a visit , from this same gentlemen who played left back or right back and my honourable and gallant friend parted with something. (Laughter). I have also a sort of sus- picion that this same noble football player fell into the hands of another friend of mine Mr. Wilson,the member for Holderness division, and he was then, I think, a Yorkshire cricketer. Unfortunately Mr. Wilson knew the full name of a cricketer named Wilson and this un- fortunate man quoted the wrong Christian name. • A policeman was sent for, and he is now, I suppose, lodg- ing at his Majesty's expense." (Laughter). COLONEL. WALKER AND TRADE_ QUESTIONS

Government did not give facilities for Sir William Hart Dyke's Bill to pass the trade must oppose the Government. Colonel Walker expressed the opinion that when magistrates declined to renewa licence they should be compelled to state their reasons for doing so; otherwise it would be impossible for the trade to find redress. Continuity", he said that public opinion had been aroused bin their favour and it was for the Government to find a remedy. They must still further provoke public indignation and not until then would they receive justice. He saw around him men of intelligence, vigour and health, and considering the calumny which had been thrown broadcast against the trade as a whole, he thought 1 it was satisfactory to con- sider that they had men of such stamina as those present to carry on the trade. As Mr. Balfour had pointed out it was.one of the most important factors that the trade should be entirely in the hands of men of means and substance, and this point should be well considered by those who dealt or intended to deal with this great social question. (Applause). • COUNCILLOR EVANS AND THE WARRINGTON

COMPENSATION TO PUBLIC-ANS.

The CHAIRMAN then proposed "The Warrington and District Licensed Victuallers' Association." At the outset he touched upon his remarks last year with refer- ence to the Licensing Act, and said he then observed that there was great danger in part two which was not fair to their trade or even to the wholesale trade. The result had been the justification of his remarks. He did not think there was any intention when that Bill was introduced that it should have any effect upon the Benches of the country. He did not think it was expected even by the promoters of the Bill that the magistrates' views would be effected in any degree by that part of the measure. He was sorry to say that it had had a great deal of effect upon some Benches,but he was happy to say that those members of4the Warrington Bench who adjudicated upon licensing matters had given in comparison with other Benches in neighbouring towns most fair-minded views in dealing with that important question. He wished it to be distinctly understood that any seyere criticisms he might pass on that occasion were not by any means directed against the members of the Warrington Bench. Although the licensed victuallers might be all right in Warrington they must remember that their association was part of other associations in this country and that unless they acted together and defended those who were attacked it would be impossible for them as an organized trade to obtain from the Government that which they desired. First of all he thought they should agitate against the present constitution of the Benches which had been somewhat unfairly built up although that was not the fault of the present Government. He was afraid that considerable political bias was brought to bear in the administration of matters brought before the magistrates. He went so far as to say that some justices wished to produce political influence against the Government for bringing forward the Licensing Bill by punishing the trade whenever they could. He was afraid that such action had a tendency to drive away those moderate men whom they desired to see officiating as magistrates. He hoped therefore that the organization of the trade would aim at a, more fitting tribunal. It *as very ques- tionable ,whether some benches were acting within the law. He maintained that magistrates in dealing with the complicated questions which were brought before them should listen to the voice of the man who led them—the Lord Chancellor. Putting aside the question of immoderate men who filled the Bench in some places, he would ask them to consider the policy ot the Benches as a whole. No doubt many, if not all, were imbued with anhonest desire to benefit social life, and an intention to promote temperance. He main- tained that the licensed victuallers also were desirous of promoting temperance—(hear, hear)—and that there was no one in the country who could promote it as the licensedtvictuallers could. h was not in the public-houses where drunkenness took place, but where drink Was obtained surreptitiously. The Bench said tit re were far too many houses, and that fewer houses the less intem- perance there would be. That was an entirely fallacious idea. He had before him some figures which had been obtained on behalf of the county magistrates of Lanca- shire. It appeared that in Birkdale,a highly respectable place, there were 784 inhabitants to each licensed house, and the number of convictions per house was 6; Seaforth, inhabitants 609, convictions 10 ; Waterloo, 480 inhabitants, convictions 8; Ormskirk, inhabitants, 232, convictions 2: Wigan, 220 inhabi- tants per house, conviction 1. He thought the figures showed that the fewer bouses the less intemperance was an entirely fallacious view. Colonel Walker salunitted figures in regard to spirit drinking which showed that in Gothenburg, where the state had taken control of the public houses, there were 7,812 people to each house and the number of convic- tions was 58 per 1,000. It had been ascertained with re- gard to Scotland that in Perth there were 670 inhabitants perhouse and only 11 convictions, and in Dundee there were 740 inhabitants per house with 14 convictions. When the magistrates said that to do away with houses must promote temperance they were acting entirely on false grounds and were doing a great evil against the promotion of temperance. Of course, evil doers must suffer and some good might have been done by the magis- trates, but it was impossible to carry out the policy of confiscation without inflicting serious injustice upon some. It was for that association to ally itself with other similar organizations and not to be satisfied until the Government took upon itself to provide—next session he thought it must be—a Compensation Bill. Colonel Walker explained Mr. Butcher's Bill and said it was not intended that the measure should pass into law, but it had been ascertained on behalf of the trade which members of Parliament were in favour of compensation. They had before them the name of every member who voted for the measure and it be- hoved them to mark those men who voted against the principle of compensation. (Applause.) The Bill would not have been acceptable to the trade in his opin- ion, and he hoped it would not go any further. What they would accept and what they must try and enforce was that the Bill produced by Sir William Hart Dyke should pass this Session. The Bill merely stated that the action of the magistrates should be suspended until January Ist 1905, which meant that no more public house should be confiscated until a Bill had been in- troducei by the Government dealing with compensation (Hear, hear.) The Government knew that the trade were aroused, that they had beat them in more than one election already, and that they meant business. If the

BENCH.

In responding Councillor JOHN EVANS said the trade had been very fairly dealt with by the Warrington Bench. At the same time he thought they might claim some credit for the mannerin whichthey had conducted the business. With one unfortunate exception they had not during the past seven years hada single case brought against any of their members for offences against the Licensing Laws. __(Applause),__ _

===== 0002601_19030515_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
THE -00UNTYs tOROUGH MAGISTRATES

This toast was proposed by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., who said he thought they might all congratulate themselves upon the fact that they had no fault whatever to find with the Warrington Bench. That they met every question with fairness he thought would be ad- mitted by everybody, and he hoped they would live long to dispense justice. It might interest them to know that the Warrington Bench could boast of perhaps the oldest magistrate in England, Mr. Holbrook Gaskell having been a member for half a century. He hoped the moderate policy which the Warrington magistrates followed would enable them all to attain the ripe old age of ninety and to sit on the 13ench as long as Mr. Holbrook Gaskell had done. (Applause). Alderman FRANCOMB responded and said he believed the magistrates had given the licensing question their most careful consideration and had tried in every way to do what was right. Alderman Francomb alluded to the alterations to houses which the magistrates had suggested and said the brewers had met the Bench in a broad-minded manner. During the last few months they had heard a great deal about the taking away of licences. The Licensing Committee had gone round and had,he believed,met; fairly anyone who had a grievance or against whom they had a grievance. The result was that in Warrington he believed they had settled the licensing question pretty nearly to the entire satisfaction of the licenced victuallers and brewers. In regard to the composition of the Bench,he believed that if a pronounced teetotaler were allowed to sit on the Licensing Committee the same privelege should be extended to the licensed victuallers. There was certainly a sufficient number of broad-minded men in the town to deal with the licens- ing question without resorting to either extreme. Why on earth they should take a lioence from a man without compensating him he did not know. They could do with a few houses being remodelled in Warrington and he believed some of them were going to be remodelled. His contention hadalways been that if they had good premises and good tenants it was a great source of promoting temperance. Fortunately in Warrington they had not a complaint against a licensedi victualler or even against an ordinary publican an d he did not think they could find a more respectable body of men con- nected with any other trade than the licensed victuallers. So long as these men carried on their business properly he did not think they had anything to fear from magis- trates, Chief Constables, or policemen. Proceeding, Alderman Francorab contended that it was not fair that the whole Bench of magistrates should be summoned to deal with a case against a licensed victualler, whereas if there was a murder trial only the magistrates who were supposed to attend that day would receive notice. He had certainly made up his mind that whether he could attend any other time or not, if ever a man holding a licence were summoned he would put himself to the greatest possible inconvenience to be present on the Bench. He believed he would be perfectly justified in doing so. He hoped the Bench would long be spared to carry out the law as they had done in the past year and that they would be able to congratulate themselves each year upon the happy results of the Licensing Sessions. MR. A. H. CROSFIELD AND WORKINGMEN'S

===== 0002601_19030515_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
CLUBS.

Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD -pr-o-p-o-s-edithe health of "The Chairman" and congratulated Colonel Walker not only on his urbanity and dignity but on the marked capacity he had shown in discussing, even from his own point of view, questions concerning the trade. He had long known by reputation the business capacity which Colonel Walker was well known to possess, but they must admire the way in which he had devoted himself not only to his own important business— great as was the position The held in that trade— but the way in which he had shown public spirit in his military and political activity. There werepeonle who took a pessimistic view of the public spirit of this nation; they said it was on the wane. He (Mr. Cros- field) was a little bit of an optimist. He did not believe that as years went en there was less public spirit or less feeling of one's duty to the State in tb old coun- try of ours. He dare say they all recor .Jted what Mark Twain said at a literary. dim, e when he had to respond to a literary ton, lie took a very pessimistic view of th, He said "Gentlemen, literature is in a bad way. Shakespeare is dead, Tennyson is dead, and I'm feel- ing verylseedy." (Laughter.) They continually heard similar pessimistic views in regard to other depart- ments, but it would be a very bad thing for England if the public spirit of those who happened to have achieved important positions was not shown by their willingness to act in other capacities than the business which concerned their own pockets. He ventured to congratulate the chairman on the public spirit he had shown!in various departments of his career.He should like to say another word or two beforesitting down, even if he should be for a moment a little discursive in doing so. Even if that should be so, he was only following the example of his friend who did have a little friendly dig at him just now. (Laughter.) It was not the first occasion on which Mr. Pierpoint had done so, and he (Mr. Crosfield) might tell them at once—speaking entirely between themselves—that although Mr. Pier- point did on one occasion rather severely upset his little applecart— (laughter) notwithstanding that passing contretemps he was happy to say the relations between his old frier' 1 and himself were certainly no worse than they were before—(applause) He was not sure that he might not add with- out any affectation or humbug, that as they had known each other more, those relations were even more cordial and more kindly than they were before. Mr. Crosfield expressed regret that he had been unavoidably prevented from attending previous gatherings and thanked the committee and president (Councillor John Evans) for their courtesy in inviting him to that banquet. Continuing, Mr. Crosfield alluded to evenings for many years past which he had spent under Mr. Allison's hospitable roof, and the many friends and members of the Licensed Victuallers'Association whom he had met there. While they had much good fellow- ship and good cheer he had never once during all that time seen a single man there overstep the mark or be the worse for liquor. Even on nights of jollification when there were victories to celebrate they had always known how to blend hilarity with discretion, and he felt very sure that temperance friends of his in Warrington would be pleased to know that fact. The Cemetery Hotel, which he knew from many visits there, and other leading establishments of the same kind were in fact what a good many licensed premises only professed to be in name, that was to say real workingmen's clubs. That was what he thought they should aim at; clubs where the sottish folly of mere soaking was no more tolerated than it would be in the most exclusive club in London. He would not venture, it would be presumption in the presence of so much expert knowledge if he did venture to dogmatise on the question as to how many of such clubs should be maintained in a community like War- rington, and he would prefer on that point to refer them to authorities like Bishop Jayne. But without entering into that matter he felt most strongly that it was on those lines that an ultimate solution of what was termed the problem of" the trade" would best'command—and rightly command—public sympathy and support. The CHAIRMAN responded, and in thanking Mr. Pier- point for his attendance, referred to the manner in which that gentleman discharged his Parliamentary duties. He might safely say that he (Colonel Walker) was never in the House of Commons without seeing his friend there also. Colonel Walker also thanked Sir Gilbert Greenall, Mr. Mortimer, Mr. Crosfield, and Alderman Francomb for their attendance. Speaking on the question of alterations to licensed premises, he said there was no doubt that there were many properties which could be improved beneficially both for the public and the tenants. At times, however, alterations might be carried to an extreme,. but he hoped the question would always be dealt With fairly.

CHURCH OF THE SACRED HEART.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield Opens the New Club.

The young men attending the Church of th:-. S tiered Heart are now provided with excellent recreative facili- ties,a club having recently been opened in the building adjoining the church and furnished with a capital billiard table. So far, the innovation has met with en- couragingsuccess, many members having been enrolled. and on Thursday lastiweek the club was formally opened by Mr. A. H. Crosfield, who afterwards played a billiard match with Mr. M. Connor. The Rev. Father Ryan pre- sided over a very good attendance and was supported by the Rev. Father Quirk, of Widnes. The Rev. Father RYAN said it did not need any words from him to introduce Mr. Crosfield who, he supposed, was about the best-known man in Warrington, and de- servedly so. In his kindness he had come amongst them that evening not only to give them an exhibition of billiards but to formally, open their little club. They would be ever grateful to him for his kindness and they thanked him sincerely for it. They had gone to a little trouble in opening the club for the welfare of the young men of the districtand during the short time that it had been openedlit had been very well patronized. He was sure Mr. Crosfield and everybody knew how good it was for young men te avail- themselves of such a place as that, rather than other places which he would not men- tion. It gave him very great pleasure to introduce Mr. Crosfield. (Applause.) Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, who was cordially received, said he thanked them very much for their kindness in asking him to come and play a game of billiards. The French had an old saying " Qui s'excuse s'accuse " and he had been making excuses very much about his form at billiards, which he was afraid was at present very rocky. After receiving the invitation to open the club, which his old friend Mr. Willett conveyed to him —and which being conveyed by him, he (Mr. Crosfield) dare not for a moment refuse—he thought it would only be prudent to have a little bit of practice on the quiet at Hoylake where they had excellent tables and every opportunity. Unfortunately since he got back to Eng- land about ten days ago, he had really not had even a spare half hour in the evening to devote to billiards, and he had had no opportunity of making up for lost time. It was certainly twelve if not eighteen months since he played a game of billiards at all. He expected his friend Mr. Connor would give him a very severe drubbing, and that he (Mr. Crosfield) would wind up in that condition which was once described in Parliament as being smashed, de- molished, and pulverised. (Laughter.) But even if that were so, it would be a great pleasure to him to have assisted in the opening of that club,—and for a good many years past it had given him very unqualified pleasure to assist in a small degree in furthering recreation such as could be obtained at any club of that kind, and also outdoor retreation in Warrington. (Applause.) They heard nowadays of what was called intensive culture. That was usually applied to agriculture,but there was another form of intensive culture which he thought some of them experienced, and that was the intensive culture of their own brains. With the demands which by their business, public life or other occupations were made upon people nowadays, he thought it became more necessary that outdoor sports and the means of recrea- tion such as were to be found in a club of that kind were maintained, supported and encouraged in all the great industrial centres in the country they had Lord Rosebery asking hwas nowdoing in an important article "What is use of having an empire without an Imperial race ?" It was very important that the means of healthy recrea- tion should be sustained and encouraged in order that the physique and fighting power of the good old English stock should be maintained. (Applause). He saw from the list of rules of the club that politics were barred, and he dare say that if he who discreet he should prob- ably proceed at once to receive the very severe licking that he knew was awaiting him at the board of the green cloth, but before receiving that terrible castigation— (laughter)—he would like to thank them for asking him to come there. He appreciated the invitation very much, and he would like to tell them that, in the old days when his father lived at Walton—(applause)—for a great many years there were no more honoured guests at Walton Lea than the reverend Catholic fathers of this town. He thought that was rather a pleasing thing to consider for one moment, because he dare say they all knew that his father was born a Quaker,and retained all his life a good many of the instincts and precepts of Quakers. In point of ritual and ceremonial Quakers from a religious point of view, were almost as wide apart as the poles from the ceremonial of the Catholic Church but that did not prevent the reverend fathers in War- rington being close friends of his father in his day. (Applause). He (Mr. Crosfield) was supposed to inherit a little of the Quaker tradition, although there were some candid friends—and anyone who was so foolish or whojhadthe temerity to meddle with politics wasalways certain to have a few candid friends—who told him that he had not inherited all the severity and austerity which was understood to characterise some of the old Quaker stock from which he was descended, but he dare say he had a little bit of it, aud even that had not pre- vented him forthe past seventeen years from working in , a political capacity alongside of good Irish Catholic] friends of his in Warrington who commenced in 1886 to advocate the policies which were then very unpopular butwhich, he was sure it must rejoice the hearts of his Catholic friends to notice were now bearing important, valuable and permanent fruition. He thought that was an instance of the toleration, the mutual for- bearance, and broad-mindedness on matters of religion which was one of the most precious heritages of old England. (Applause.) He thought they had had a most marked and memorable instance of that—far above all party politics, but one which would not be forgotten and which was not the least important instance I —in a journey that was full of great and permanent results, which had just been made by his Majesty the King. (Applause.) The fine broad-minded spirit which the King evinced on all occasions was shown—and he (Mr. Crosfield) thought it would be appreciated all the world over—when they had the memorable' and he would I even say touching meeting between his Majesty the ' King and the Venerable Pontiff in Rome. (Applause.) He thought that had been a lesson to all of them, and reverting to their own town he thought it was most kind of them to ask him, although he was not exactly a Quaker now, but still an ex-Quaker, to open that club and to make a willing sacrifice of himself in the match he was about to play. (Applause.) Mr. O'BRIEN proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Cros- field, and said he looked upon his visit to the club as a great honour to the members. Mr. Crosfield did not agree with them from a religious point of view, but he had shown that he had a kindly heart and that the days of bigotry were gone. (Applause.) They lived in an age of liberty when a Catholic had the same chance of push- ing forward in this world as a man who was a Protes- tant. He was sure the members of the club would never forget Mr. Crosfield's kindness, and they hoped he would be victorious in the billiard match. (Applause.) The Rev. Father QUIRK seconded and said he had never met a gentleman who had shown more tolerance and equity than Mr. Crosfield had done. The motion was heartily agreed to and Mr. Crosfield briefly responded, remarking that lie devoutly and sincerely wished it were possible for him to make a better return for theirkindness. (Applause.l The billiard match was then proceeded with and in- ' stead of receiving a "terrible castigation "Mr. Cros- fieldcame off victorious by a margin of 28. The match was for 200 up andMr.Crosfield made the winning stroke when his opponent had reachedl72. His highest break as 31, whilst Mr. Connor's best effort was 26.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must inall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday.

Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one.

We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake to return the manuscripts which are not used.

===== 0002601_19030515_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
Compensation to Brewers, Publicans,

and Slaveowners.

At the Licensed Victuallers' gathering last week the question of Compensation for the disappointed expecta- tion of the renewal of a licence, filled a large space in the addresses. One of the speakers drew a touching pictare,of the trade, and its burden of excessive taxation. In extending our sympathy to this sorely harassed industry, it is well to remember two things, first, that the taxation is paid ultimately not by the trade, but in the price of the beverages of those who drink, secondly, that these taxes are largely used in recovering the salvage from the human wreckage wrought by intemper- ance. We need not attempt to fix the portion of poor' rates, police charges, prison and asylum expenses, due to excessive drinking All agree that drunkenness is the prime factor in creating these burdens. It is quite true, that the legitimate and moderate use of these beverages should not necessarily entail the abuse, which in the end causes such heavy public burdens, and so much social degrad ation. But we have to, deal with what is, rather than what should be, and the fact that drinking and drunken- ness with all their attended evils, and financial charges, grow upon the same tree, and appear inseparable, ren- ders restrictive measures inevitable. In justice to those engaged in the liquor traffic, I would say that as a whole they are as much opposed to drunkenness as other por- tions of the community and exert themselves in the direction of moderation and sobriety, and in this direetion are a powerful factor too little recognized. As far as restraint and moderation can prevent drunkenness, those engaged in the distribution of intoxicants can exert a powerful influence. In the case of those (ad unhappily in this England of ours there are very many) whose only safety is abstinence this gospel of moderation has no message. But passing to the question of compensation for dis- appointed expectancy, Mr. Robert Pierpoint M.P. drew an analogy between this and the compensation given to slaveowners when this country liberated the slaves. A more unfortunate analogy to enforce his point, could scarcely be found. This can best be realized if we con- sider the differences in the two cases. The slaveowner paid full value for his slaves: The publican pays nothing for his yearly license. The slaveowners acquired by purchase his slaves without reservaticn or restriction. They were his for life, and tie fruit of their labour and their body was the absolute property of their master. There is no property in a licence beyond a year. It is a monopoly granted for public conveni- ence, free of cost, for one year. When the slaves were liberated, the State paid the owners for what they themselves had expended. Mr. Pierpoint would have the State compensate a licensee for that for which he had paid nothing. It is the monopoly which gives to licenses their value. If we had free trade in the sale of liquor, as we have in the case of bread, and chipped potatoes, no special value would exist. The licensee does not create this special value, but the community by curtailing the right of sale to a limited number. If any recognition is to be made of benefit received, the licensee ought to pay the community for placing him in the position of vantage, for however many years the community care to renew his license and preventing new competitors from invading his domain. The kernel ' of the whole thing lies in the answer we give to the question. Does the State restrict the sale of liquor for the benefit of the public or the publican? Leaving Mr. Pierpoint and his inappropriate analogy, we would pass to the proposal made by Mr. W. B. Roberts on behalf of the trade. His plan is that the amount of compensation should be the totalnf five years receipts, half to be paid by the State out of taxes on beer &c(which by the way are already largely used in dealing with the fruits of the traffic) and the other half, to be paid by the ratepayers. Ido not know what the average weekly takings of a public house are, but I should fancy that a publican who took £2O a week would not consider that he had possession of a very thirsty corner. This would rnean £lOlO a year, 0r£5200 in five years, and would constitute the amount Mr. Roberts thinks ought to be given as compensation, halt out of the taxes, and half out of the rates. Of the whole the owner is to get l 0 per cent, the brewer 50 per cent, and the licensee 20 per cent. A measure framed on these this would be me re a brewer's compensation bill than a publican's,

===== 0002601_19030515_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
Manchester Port Sanitary Authority. The text of a Bill conferring a provisional Order varying the constitution of the Manchester Port Sani- tary Authority was issued on Wednesday. According to the original order the Corporation of Widnes and the Rural District Councils of Chester and Wirral were formed into one district, which was entitled to one thirteenth of the membership of the authority, and for the purpose of electing this proportion a joint com- mittee had to be elected, consisting of five representa- tives from Widnes, two from the Chester Rural Dis- trict Council, and one from Wirral. It has been found that the Ellesmere Port and Whitby Cheshire Rural District Council is a riparian authority in itself, and, therefore, it is proposed to take one of the represen- tatives from the Chester Rural District Council, and give one to the Ellesmere Port and Whitby Rural District Council., • -

UNDER the dr.ocic or GOOD BOOTS.—Advt. FIRE GRATES of every description.—J. S. GAR- Lam), Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street,Warringion.—Advi.

H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. A Collision in the Ship Canal. In the Liverpool County Court, yesterday week, his Honour Judge Collier, sitting with nautical assessors, had before him an action arising out of a collision in. the Ship Canal. The plaintiffs were the Zillah Ship- ping and Carrying Company, Limited, owners of the steamer Sarah Brough, and they claimed £3OO from .the owners of tha steamship Millicent. The defen- dants counterclaimed for a similar amount. Mr. A. D. ,Bateson: appeared for the plaintiffs, and Mr. Leslie Scott was counsel for the defendants. It was stated by Mr. Scott that the defendants had tendered "both to blame," but the plaintiffs would not accept it. John Lewis, the master .of the Sarah Brough, and several other members of the crew were called, and described how the collision was brought about. According to their story, the Sarah Brough was in January last on a passage from Widnes to Fleetwood in ballast, and when in the .Manchester I, Ship Canal, somewhere in the vicinitYof Ince Catting, she met the Millicent going in the opposite direction. The-two vessels collided, and it was alleged by the plaintiffs that the Millicent was alone to blame for the casualty as she admittedly sheered over to her wrong side of the Canal. The defendants admitted that the Millicent was not on her proper side of the waterway,but contended that the collision would have been avoided had the Sarah Brough stopped and reversed when sh saw the other vessel run in towards the bank.. Without hearing the defendants' evidence, the court held that the Sarah Brough was to blame for the collision. His Honour stated that the nautical assessors and himself were of opinion that it was the duty of the Sarah Brough to have stopped and re- versed directly those on board her saw the Millicent going in towards the bank. Judgement was accord- ingly given for the defendants the damages being re- ferred to the Registrar.

The Rev. B. Percival and Passive Resistance. The school sermons were preached at St. Peter's Church on Sunday, in the morning by the Rev. B. Percival, vicar, and in the evening by the Rev. S. Wil- kinson, vicar of Timperley. In the afternoon a special men's service was held, when the address was given by the Curate, the Rev. D. R. Jones. The collections, which were in aid of the schools, amounted to £5 18s. 20. In his sermon in the morning the Vicar said that previous to 1870 the Government had done nothing for education, the Church having chiefly educated the poor. In 1870, however, the Government took up the matter by the introduction of Mr. Forster's Act, and since thattime increased aid had been given in the shape of school fees, and ultimately the 17s. 6d. limit was done away with, enabling all schools ko have what they really earned whether they had a large income or not. Then came the .recent Act, which had probably caused more talk than any Act which had been passed since 1870. He confessed that he had no sympathy with the passive resistance policy because there had been no change in the principle of education; the same principle had held good since the passing of the first Act. That was to say that money was received through Imperial taxation, whereas now it was going to be placed on the ordinary rates. The only difference was that there was representation under the present system, whereas there was none under the other one. Education must not be divorced from religion. That was what was meant by education. It was to pro- duce mental power, moral worth, and religious character. The education of the poor must give them moral and religious•worth, strengthen their minds, raise them above the almost brute life they led to the higher life of the spirit, and make them free like the Israelites. Workingmen's Mission Sunday School.

The Sunday School anniversary sermons at Bank Quay were preached on Sunday,by Mr. Richezd Hatton, of Winsford. In the morning before a good congrega- tion, he took for his text, "Christ and the children." His sermon was one of interest all through. He said he firmly believed in child conversion, and one of his best points was that if a child knowingly did a wrong, it was old enough to be taught to do right, and so he Wight of the mysteries of God. The choir and children, rendered their pieces well, andshvwed that their con- ductor, Mr. Fred Atkinson, must have gone to a lot of trouble to train them so well. In the afternoon, a service of song,was rendered by the children, and choir entitled "Won by a Gipsy," the connective readings, being admirably read by Mrs. Simpson, of Warrington. The chapel was crowded to excess, many having to be content to sit in the vestry. All the pieces were well rendered by the children, and choir, and many thanks are given to those who came to;help to make the service a success. In the evening,before a crowded congregation the preacher tdok for his text "Sowing and Reaping,' Too much praise, cannot be given to Mr. Hatton for the way he handled his text. The children and Choir rendered their pieces well. The collections realised ell 3s. -lid. for which the committee feel very thankful.— In the schoolroom Thewlis-street, on Monday night, a lecture was given by Mr. Hatton, on the famous preacher," Squire Brookes "the chairman, being Coun- cillor James Evans. A plate was placed at the door for anyone who felt disposed to give, and the donations amounted to lie. 6d.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

===== 0002601_19030515_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
Baptist Church Golborne-street.

The Rev. H. Dunnico preached the second of a series of sermons on " Christianity and several questions" on Sunday evening. There was a orowded congregation, including a large preponderance of men. Having I replied to several letters with reference to the previous week's sermon he said that very briefly he wished them to look at "Competition in the light of Christianity." He was afraid that Christian men who recognized the law of Christ in their personnl life, when they entered the world of commercial enterprise, were not guided by a'hrist's law, but by what he would term natural law or the law of competition. Again and again he had conversed with good men who had attempted to justify a certain course of action by saying "Business is bu2ine3s and cannot be conducted on the principles of benevolence." Much to their re- gret they left outside their business what they admitted to be the authoritative principle of their personal and private 'life. Competition, however, like all the principles of naturehad its good and beneficent sideand looking at it in the light of social evolution they must admit that it had played a necessary part and on the whole had plaTed that part well. He did not think, however, that it was possible to define competition in a way that would harmonize with the teaching of Christ. He did not however, denounce it as , unchristian, up to a certain point no more unchristian ' than the laws of chemistry but there was a point when it did become decidedly unchristian and it was at that point it must be resisted. So long as the great object of competition was to develope the natural resumes of the earth and to accumulate the wealth necessary for pro- gress it played a useful part but when the problem was , not to produce wealth but to distribute it, it became a band. Competition in;product ion was good but compet ion in the opportunity to produce was wrong because at that point it ceased to be a principle of progress and become a principle of mutual destructiofi. There was no doubt that the reckless competition of modern times was largely due to the teachings of physical science. As men plunged into competition disregardless of the effect it might have upon others they did so not because they were brutal but because they had some dim notion that they were following out the laws of nature and acting in conformity with the great principle of the Universe. Competition however was not the only factor in the evolution of things nor was it the most important. Prof. Huxley for instance, had admitted that moral progress was affected not by falling into line with the principle of nature but by a certain resistance to nature. At the creation of the moral sense the order of nature was reversed and the moral law asserted itself, not by yielding to but by resisting the nature inwhich it lived. In concluding, Mr. Dunnico said unlimited competition was wrong. Every man had right to earn his bread and it was the duty of the State to see that he had it. Whenever an industry became a necessity that industry belonged to the nation, and up to a certain point the state was responsible for the physical and moral welfare of its people.—Next Sunday Mr. Dunnico will speak upon the "Poverty of England: Its causes, itg remedies."

The general public has little idea of the amount of detail and labour involved in advertising an exhibition of the character and magnitude of the Buffalo Bill Wild West which is announced to exhibit here on Monday, May 25th. It may therefore be interesting to give a few particulars of this most important branch of the great organization. To begin with, it requires a staff of some forty-five ageiSs and bill-posters tolproperly accomplish this task. These men travel from city to city in their own specially constructed car. This car is provided with sleeping bunks and every accommodation for the comfort of these hard workers while travelling, for it is I their home during their long travels and journeys I from city to city which are made during the night. In one end of this travelling work-shop is an elabor- ately furnished office from which the car manager issues all his instructions to the men regarding their respective duties for the following day, a task necessitating a thorough knowledge of all the railways centreing into a city and the various suburban towns which are ant on the lines, and can only be reached by waggons. Arrangements having been made in advance for the running of special excursions to the various points at which exhibitions are to be given, a number of men are dispatched upon the first trains leaving north, east, west and south whose duty it is to secure prominent hoard- ings in all of the towns and villages along the lines for a distance of some fifty miles, and cover them with posters announcing the coming of the exhibition. Another batch of men known as country billp3sters" take waggons,whichjhave been contracted for in advance and arrive at the station at day-break, and drive over the various country roads, in every direction, posting pictorial paper upon barns, fences and every other avail- able place where permission can be obtained, for a dis- tance of some twenty miles. Other gangs qf men, known as "city bill-posters "load their paste cans'and printing into another set of waggons and proceed to cover the various hoardings within the city limits, while still others are occupied in obtaining consent from the various shop-keepers to place attractive lithographs and photographic cards in the windows or affix specially provided boards to the sides of buildings, while equally important is the task of the men who go from house to house distributing various sorts of illustrated descrip- tive matter recounting the interesting facts and features of the show. Thus, each in their own way, contribute to the successful advertising of the exhibition upon ja scale of magnitude which can only be accomplished by the most thorough organization and !perfect system. Along the centre of the advertising car are large lockers where hundreds of different kinds of posters are systematically arranged, above which are long tables where the men attach the small slips giving the name of the city and date of the exhibition's visit. In the other end of the car is an immense boiler which is used for the manufacture of the daily consumption of paste. At four o'clock every morning the men leave their bunks and proceed with the manufacture of the pa.ste.This accomplished and the paste cans all filled, it is time for breakfast, at the con- clusion of which the real work of the day begins. The car remains in each city the same length of time as the show is advertised to give performances, whether it be three weeks, one week or 4 single clay, and always travels exactly twenty-one days in advance of such announced dates. Rain or shine the work must be carried on just the same, for once a day is lost it is a very difficult task to catch it up again, so despite the conditichis of the elements this small army of hard workers between them succeed in posting from fifteen to twenty thousand sheets of paper before the labour is completed. During the past few weeks, and at the present time, this great show has been exhibiting in Liverpool, from whence the special trains,with 800 men and 500 horses will arrive at Warrington by daylight on Sunday morning, the 25th inst. The stay in Liverpool has been a huge success, among the visitors being the Bishop of Liver- pool who, on Sunday last held a Church Service in the show grounds at Edge-lane. The weather was atrocious and when the Bishop delivered his beautiful address he approached closely to the congregation, and was prac- ticallyunsheltered from the pitiless rainfall other than the protection he derived from an umbrella hold over his head and shoulders by Major Burke, the general manager of the show. It is scarcely necessary to men- tion that throughout as service his lordship,who(asalso were the other clergy) was surpliced, remained bare- headed, and at the close of the proceedings he had the appearance of being very rain-soaked. That he will suffer no ill-effects for his remarkable intrepidity will be the earnest prayer of all who admired his courage and steadfastness of purpose. The Bishop commenced his discourse by saying: "Colonel Cody and my friends, it is a singular honour and pleasure my being* here to speak to you as an Englishinan. Between England and the United States of America I trust there will never be anything else but peace, for we worship God in the same faith and in the same language, and as a bishop of the National Church, I came hereto speak to you a few words on be- half of our Master—Christ." His lordship then reminded them that years ago he made their acquaintance when they were performing at Earl's , Court, London, and he then realised, as he did now, the great skill and courage they must have shown in their former frontier life. He was glad to see them again to express his gratitude for what he had then seen and heard, and which he had never forgotten; it was an instructive lesson to him. His lordship then said he was going to address them on the words uttered by the patriarch Job, many thousands of years ago— " Behold. God is mighty and dispises not anything," and in doing so he (the bishop) said he felt sure he was not addressing anyone present who did not in some way have belief in God. The address his lordship delivered was singularly eloquent, but simple in language. However humble or small an individual might be, we were all sure of God's care and pro- tection, for He never forgot—the creature might forget his Maker, but the Maker never forgot that which He had created. Those present who had passed through many great trials and dangers were there to-day as witnesses and monuments of God's greatness. In concluding his discourse his lordship said he might never see them again, but if we put our trust in God the Father we should meet at last in that great home where temptation was never felt. The scene, notwithstanding the inclement weather, was very picturesque. Colonel Cody (who welcomed the Bishop) and his niece occupied one of the front seats and around him were grouped his Indian warriors and their squaws and children, dressed in their fantastic blankets, &c. They followed the service with much interest, and it was noticeable that many of the dusky chiefs joined in the hymn singing. Other principle show officials were Major J. M. Burke, Messrs. Johnnie Baker L. E. Decker, Charles S. Wells, D. W. Fellows, &c.

BUFFALO',-)BILL'S WILD

WEST.

THE FORTHCOMING VISIT.

ABOUT THE SHOW.

It is almost fticlei-n-a-ttenipt to enumerate all the in- teresting features of the great Wild West show. Of course the most prominent individual is Colonel William F. Cody, or Buffalo Bill" as he is better known, a picturesque figure in contemporaneous history that will never see his prototype. Scenes and incidents of every variety of Wild West life will be produced withpositive fidelity to nature, and many of the stirring events of American frontier life of half a century ago will be re- enacted. In addition to the Wild West representations the Congress of Rough Riders of the World will attract almost equal interest. In this Congress will appear Vaqueros from Mexico, Gauchos from South America, Arabs from the African desert and Cossacks from the steppes of Russia. These latter, hardy and dexterous horsemen, may be considered as on the border line between the wild irregu- lars and the trained and drilled military; for though they wear the well-known uniform, and are veterans from the regularly enlisted soldiers among the Cossacks of the Caucasian line, they ride with a wild, fierce dash and individuality not characteristic of any of the other bodies of cavalrymen. This does not mean that they are not well drilled in their tactics and capable of going through their evolutions admirably; but they have a certain sort of spirited freedom and capacity for independent personal demonstration which only perhaps the British and Americans might, if encouraged thereto, display. They wear handsome but sombre uniforms, ride the lithe, small horses of the steppes, and are mounted Pn small troop saddles, very high front and rear, which are built up vvith pads fully four inches thick, so that they seem to perch far above the horses. A man must be a good rider who can occupy that aerial roost and steady himself in the toy stirrups which are only big enough to get the points of the toes into, and drawn short. But the Cossack not onlyldoes that, but much more. With his horse at full gallop he swoops down and picks up a handkerchief from the ground, holds fast by the saddle and seems to float in the air alongside, swings himself back to his seat and throwing his feet straight up in the air rests on his shoulders, or head, upon the saddle and finally crossing his stirrups over the saddle stands upright with his toes ii them,and all this without diminishing his headlong pace. Personally the Cossacks are slender, lithe men, wiry, tough and gritty, making as little account of per- sonal damage as ever the cowboys would. Another variety of horsemen which forms an auxiliary to the other attractions of the Wild West are the representa- tive cavalrymen of several of the great militarynations. The entire entertainment is unique and `remarkable in every respect, and will be seen at Warrington, behind the Causeway Hotel, complete in every detail, on May 25th.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

The Warringtoniaas ,naintained their unbeaten record on Saturday, when Bootle suffered defeat on the Arpley enclosure. The weather was very gloomy all afternoon, but the rain kept off until the evening. It was, however, necessary to draw stumps before the home eleven had completed their innings, but the Warringtonians had held possession of the field long enough to enable them to claim the victory. The wicket was in a sodden condition in consequence of the miserable weather of the previous week, and none of the batsmen attained very striking totals.

The efforts of the Bootle eleven were productive of the small aggregate of 28, the batting being of a very mediocre description. H. Williams was the top scorer and succeeded in compiling 10. Lynes again shone in the bowling department, but of course the state of the ground lent him material assistance. There can be no gainsaying the fact that under any circum- stances his work was remarkably creditable, no fewer than -7 wickets being accounted for at the small cost of 9 runs. J. W. Bibby was also in good form, and accounted for 3 wickets at a cost of 17 runs.

The home team made a disastrous start, and when Lynes and Bibby became as.sociated 4 wickets were down for ,2 runs. Lynes soon put a different com- plexion on the match, his efforts being responsible for 15. Mention must also be ,made of T. Houghton's acceptable contribution of 8 runs. J. W. Bibby had the misfortune to be caught by Bouchier after knock- ing up 2. .As regards the Bootle bowling' Mason cap- tured 6 wickets for 21 runs, and Sandiford 2 wickets for 5 runs. Warrington had put together 33 for the loss of 8 wickets when a driizling rain set in and stumps were drawn.

Sankey Wire Mills fared badly on Saturday, the Lyinin eleven easily defeating them by a margin of 51. Lowe (26) and Marsden (16) were the moit successful batsmen on the winning side, whilst F. Taylor (13) was the top scorer for the Wire Mills. Thorpe bowled very effectively for the visitors, Capturing 7 wHzets for 12 runs.

Penketh disposel of Hough Green by a majority of 8, with 3 wickets in hand, 35 and 27 being the res pective totals. W. Crosskill (19) rendered valuable service with the bat, being the only player to attain double figures.

Rugby Football.

The annual meeting of the football club will beheld on • Tuesday evening, when the secretary's and treasurer's reports will be submitted, and officers for the following year elected. Most members wit, I think, be agreed that the reports, particularly the treasurer's,are surprisingly satisfactory. The club is in an excellent financial plsition, the abstract of accounts showing liabilities, nil, and assets, V,256' 17s. sd. There is something like £570 cash available—a nice little nest egg for the purchase of forwards and other useful things in the future. The annual sports alone realised :tll3 profit, which is a handsome testimonial to the popularity of the event and the geniality of the weather on that particular occasion. Gate-money during the season realised the handsome sum of £1692, and the " A " team gate alone was ;en in ex- cess of the gross gate from "A " team matches the preceding season. Everything Considered, .members of the club should be fairly complacent next Tues- day. There is net a great deal to grumble about.

All the retiring officerS are re-nominated, and there is no opposition except for places on the committee. The names of the new candidates do not strike me very favourably, and I think it will be to the advant- age of the Club if the retiring members dre re-elected. Mr. Ashton, the secretary,had some notion of retiring I believe. Whether the notion is, still there, I do not know, but I fancy -the ,members will be prepared to promptly knock it on the head if it manifests itself at the annual meeting. They do not want to part with Mr. A.shton, and they won't if they can help it..

I see that genial " T.P." the treasurer of the club, has completed 21 years service in that office. My congratulations to him on his majority! The interior of the hall was tastefully decorated by Mr. R tlph Johnson. The material which had been used for the purpose hung in festoons from the gallery, and ahe various colours blended harmoniously. The stalls were nicely arranged round the room, and each was dis- tinguished by a distinctive colon- or colours. The colours of the school stall for instance were gold, white and mauve, the college stall had simply old gold and white, whilst Mrs. Steven- son's stall bore the national colours of red white and blue. The numerous assistants at the refresh- ment stall were distinguished by a badge of violets. The stalls displayed the goods to the best advantage but special care had been bestowed on the flower stall. This occupied a position on the platform and was a de- light to all beholders. The flowers had been arranged with artistic skill and the effect was considerably- heightened by the introduction of a number of electric glow-lamps. In addition to the usual stalls to be found at bazaars there . was an electrical fish-pond, a "Tay- kin" museum of curiosities and flneart gallery, a penny post office'with" letters for everybody and from every- body," a weighing machine where for a penny weighing was 'done while you wait." and other means for re- lieving the visitor of his cash. Those who desired could have their palms read or their bumps felt or listen to the gramophone. The entertainments were a feature of the bazaar. Not only were there grand selections of music in the large ball by Mrs. W. B. Roberts' Ladies Orchestra and Mr. R. H. Pearson's band, but there were numerous attrac- tions in the crush room, including dramatic perform- ances, costume concerts, popular variety concerts, exhibitions of fancy dancing and concerts by school children. In addition there were humorous competi- tions for ladies and gentlemen. The stalls and stall-holders were as follows: The School Stall: Mr. Amos, Miss Barker, Miss M. Timewell, assisted by Messrs. Turton,Lon€7"shaw, Tuke, Misses Gorse, Fowler, Sweetlove, Bennet, Spendelow, Clayton and Matthews. Boys school, advertisements and flowers ; girls school, plain and fancy work; infants school, toys and fancy work. Hall Coates and John A. Brookes.
