WARRINGTON TRAINING COLLEGE.

BAZAAR IN PARR HALL.

It is not often that the general public of Wa•crington are appealed to on behalf of the Training College. It is, of course, a Church of England institution, serving the three dioceses of Liverpool, Manchester, and Chester/ by young ladies for the teaching profession. Never- theless the people Of Warrington have shown a willing disposition to assist this institution which has for so long quietly performed such useful work in their midst, and evidence of this disposition was afforded on Thurs- day and again yesterday when a bazaar was hold .in the Parr Hall with the object of assisting the college in its financial difficulties. It is not necessary here to explain at any length the objects of the bazaar for they were fully set forth in the Speech delivered by the Principal at the opening ceremony on Thursday, but it may be briefly stated that the bazaar had tha two-fold object of clearing off the Practising Schools and furnishing the remainder of the money required for the transformation of Orford Hall: into an additional College Hostel. For these two *objects a sum of about £1,200 is required. H - Acting on a suggestion of the head teachers of tha practising school, the project of holding a bazaar was I actively pushed forward. A general committee was I formed and set to work immediately. The Rev. Morley I Stevenson, M.A., was appointed chairman, Mr. W. E. Atkin, hon. treasurer, Mr. T. D. Amos, hon. secretary, and the other members of the committee were: Revs. G. W. Dickenson, H. A. Lester, Messrs. W. Brookes, E. C. Cooper, R. Johnson, J. H. Fisher, IV. S. Woods, E. Draper, W. E. Atkin, Mesdames Stevenson, James Faircleugh, R. Johnson, Wilcox, Lester, Atkin, Misses Ferriman, Timewell, M. Timewell, Barker, Bell, Brookes, Perry' Bowden, Wilcox. and the Misses White. The I • completeness of the arrangements for the bazaar spoke volumes for the thoroughness with which the com- mittee did its work.

===== 0002601_19030515_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OPENING CERENIONY.

THE REV.IVIORLEY STEVENSON EXPLAINS THE OBJECTS OF THE BAZAAR.

The Rev. MORLEY STEVENSON explained at Some length the objects of the bazaar. In the first place, they were seeking to clear off the debt on the Praotising Schools in Hamilton-street. in the year 1887 there was an old school in Crossley-street which served that part of the town. The town was very much smaller then and the school buildings were so bad that they were con- demned by the Government Inspector and ordered to be closed. That was done and an order was sent down from the l3oard• of Education to form a School Board within three months. Then it was that the committee of the Training College saw an opportunity for establishing Practising schools and they stepped forward with an offer to pay half the cost of building large new schools if the other half could be raised by voluntary contributions. Through the kindness of many friends that was done and large schools in Hamilton street was then built and the]town was saved the cost of a School Board. Enough money was raised to build the schools but unfortunately not enough money was raised to furnish them, and the managers who were loath to keep the schools empty had therefore to start with a large debt upon the schools for the apparatus and furniture. That debt had practically remained and had always hampered the funds of the schools. Then there arose another contingency which he supposed the managers ought to have foreseen but which he believed many people did not always foresee in their younger days and that was the contingency of street making. If they erected at large building as a sort of island in the middle of a great waste of land, in coupe of time houses were erected round it and the character of the place changad. They would easily understand that when streets were made round the schools on four sides the managers were called upon to pay their share of the cost. If he might pass his experience on to other people he should like oto warn anybody against indulgeace in the amusement of street making. it was neither a refreshing one and it was a very expensive one. He knew the new amusement of motoring was considered to be expensive but he doubted whether it was so 'expensive as street making and he might say there was a great deal more to be said for it. ' Perhaps they would be warned by his example and not go in for street making as a recreation and amusement. (Laughter.) It was to wipe off the debit balance which had been left, even after very care- ful management, in connection with the original debt for furnishing and the street making expenses that they appealed for assistance. The second object was the ex- tension of the Training College. One effect of the pass- ing of the recant Education Act would be to create a very large demand for trained teachers—a much larger demand probably than the training colleges of the county at first would be able to cope with. Just as the committee of the Training College were realising this there fell vacant through a cause which all WarringtOn had deplored and would long continue to deplore, the very large and beautiful oldhouse known as Orford Hall and it seemed as if it would be almost wrong to allow such a chance as that to slip. Therefore the committee decided, if possible, that they would take Orford Hall and use it as an additional hostel of the Training College. They must not think that the Prin- cipal of the Training College was necessarily anxious to increase the number of his students. It might be thought that the care of even 128 students falling upon the devoted head of one man would be sufficient to turn the one hair that he might have left, and no doubt that was rapidly becoming the case. (Laughter.) But although the authorities of the college were by no means anxious for their own sake to add to their num- bers and so to add to the responsibility increased cares and innreased work,still they all felt that as a Training College the duty rested upon them to do what they could towards the further supply of the number of trained teachers whinh undoubtedly would be felt as a very pressing need before many months were over. (Hear, hear.) Consequently they had decided to take over Orford Hall. Of course the hall would have to be furnished and the existing classrooms of the college would have to be en- larged to meet the requirements of the additional num- bers. The cost of:that would be about £1,500. They would see that the committee had pot been altogether idle during the _last ,six weeks when he told them that they had raised a little over £9OO. They wanted to complete the stun required and he hoped the . result of the bazaar would go a long way in enabling them to do so. The first idea of the bazaar was due to the head teachers of the Practising Schools. They kindly came forward, and offered to do their best to help the schools. That idea was further taken up by his own staff and the college, and had been most ably supported by students, past and present, and by many kind friends. He was quite overwhelmed with the kindness they had received from all quarters. He believed that was the first time Miss Grayson had performed the opening ceremony at a bazaar in Warrington. Those who knew Liverpool would know Miss Grayson well. There was scarcely any good work in Liverpool, especially in the way of religious education, with which her name was not identified. (Hear, Hear.) MISS GRAYSON AND RELIGIOUS EDUCATION. Miss GRAYSON, who had a hearty reception, on rising

===== 0002601_19030515_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
o doclars te lmaar; open, said :—lt gives.lne much pleasure to be present to-day, and to take part in thia effort to further and ektend the good work done by the Warrington Training College. Education is one of the burning questions of the day—we are passing through 'an educational crisis—to some people the future lookS very dark, and they are very pessimistic as to the ulti- mate results of recent legislation. Ido not see whY this should be so, but think we rather ought to look. forward inn spirit of hope and confidence to the evolv- ing of an efficient uniform system of education whicil will be . productive of great national good—provided , that the Education Act be honestly carried out. The branch of education with which we have to do to-daY,--- the training of teachers—is one of V° I utmost importance. Our Liverpool Diocesan in- ' specter, in his report for 1902, writsTheeducation given in 11801001 chiefly depends on th,e qualifications of the teachers who give it and it is au important for the country that there should be a bodY of highly trained persons devoting themselves to thig work and for the cause of religious education in our schools that they should be loyal and instructed Church- men and Churchwomen. Beside this all other educa- tional problems ars relatively insignificant. These wordg I most heartily endorse. The Bishop of Winchester writing upon the subject of the religious training 0,1- teachers says :—lf they are themselves to teacu efficiently they ought themselves at a most jut- portant and receptive period of their lives to be taught systematically and wisely. Snow - ledge of their religion should not be left t‘", accident, to desultory reading, to the recollections oL childhood or to undenominational indefiniteness—in view of their future calling and responsibility the inflr ence of religious training should be thorough, accurate. and aboveall sympathetic. What the Bishop speaks el as undenominational indefiniteness seems to me to be a great danger of a good deal of religious teaching of the present day against which we should be especially or; our guard. If allowed to go on unchecked, it will leaa to very sad and serious results, and to a gradual weaker lug of our national character, for it practically renderg religious teaching ineffective. We are all conscionsii and unconsciously effected by our environment but children are especially so. It is what a teacher is in himself or herself that leaves the real impression on the; characters and lives of the children. As Churchmen ana Churchwomen we are bound to hand on to the rising- generation the faith in its purity and simplicity as has been handed down to us. It has been the source of our national greatness in the past and will be so in the future if the church is true to her great and solemn charge "Feed my sheep, feed my lambs." It is be- cause the new hostel in connection with the College ' will not only afford to an extended number of studentg high secular educational advances but will carry out the principle that definite religious teaching is neces" sary and can only be efficiently given by those wh° have themselves been so instructed that I feel it de- serves and has a right to claim most liberal and gener- ous support. I have now great pleasure in declaring the bazaar open and wishing financially it may be 5 great success.

Ask loco Two years ago," said Mrs. Webb, of South Ocken- Sports Outfitter for their Catalogue- It not procurable,appt I don, Essex, " I had an attack of influenza. It left me to 36 and 38 Queen Victoria Street, London. Factories for a long while an invalid. I was so.weak and ill that I Peckham, London; and Blmswell, Suffolk. (12 • could not stand or walk. In order to get round my room I HAD TO CRAWL ON MY HANDS AND KNEES. A doctor attended me but for months I was helpless. In the mornings I used to crawl down the stairs without using my legs and at night my husband carried me up again. One day, however, I chanced to see in a news- paper an account of a remarkable cure by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. So I bought a Vox. "After I had taken nearly the boxful my strength began to return. I lost the former feeling of weakness, and my legs got better and stronger. I persevered in taking the pills, and was, I am thankful to say, soon able to walk all over the house. "I have been better ever since. This winter, I am able, for the first time since my legs became bad, to do all my housework." Influenza is worse than any other cold; but all colds wear down the strength and thin out the blood, because any cold is a sort of fever, and fever lowers vitality. Influenza generally leaves some serious disease behind it—of the kidneys, heart, lungs, or stomach; all due to the effect on the blood. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are better than any other medicine in such cases, because they cure the cause of the trouble, which is Poor Blood and Deficient Blood. They send new and richer blood coursing through the veins with every dose. In the same way they cure Anemia, Consunlption, and all Wasting Diseases. Nor is that all. They tone-up and strengthen the system; they are good to take after a cold, but better still before it. Nothing is so good a preventive of Colds andinfluenza. But the genuine pills must be insisted upon. The shopkeeper who tries to sell something else instead, or who offers pink pills that are not Dr. Williams', should be avoided It is better to deal at honest shops where theyhand you what you ask for. If they try to sell you imitations of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills they will try to cheat you in other ways also, and you may not always find it so easy iso detect the imposition. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People bear the full name on the wrapper, and without all these seven words none are genuine. Imita- tions and substitutes never cured anything and they are very often dangerous or purgative. ,•-•' On Tuesday a meeting of this League was held at the George Hotel, Huddersfield, Mr. J. Clifford, president, in the chair. The following players were _put on and strnck off the registers: Put on—St. Helens: J. Donnelly, A. Clarke, W. Cliff e, W. Gregson, and J. T. Davies, all local. Broughton Rangers: G. Bailey, local; C. Worthington, Pendleton Britannia. Old- ham: R. Carpenter, Mountain Ash. Struck off— Broughton Rangers : R. Andersen, J.' Eve,J. H. White- head, W. H. Wilkinson, and R. Walls. The Secretary reported nominations for the League Committee as follows :—President, ' J. " Clifford., Huddersfield; vice-president, J. Platt, Oldham • hon. treasurer, J. Nicholl, Halifax; hon. secrdary, J. H. Hough- ton, St. Helens; and J. E. Warren, Warrington. Committee: First Di Asion representatives (two required) : F. Lister, Bradford; J. Goodall, Batley ; R. Walmsley, Salford; T. D. Harrison, Htmslet ; J. E. Kirkham, Broughton Rangers; J. W. Scholes, Swinton; and J. E. Warren, Warrington. Second Division repre- sentatives (two required) : It. Collinge, Rochdale Hornets, end J. B. Cooke, Wakefield Trinity. The Late Mr. E. W. T. Richmond.—The Insur- ance Claim. Mr. Duke K.C., M.P., Recorder of Davenport, gave his award on Saturday in the arbitration between the executors of the late Mr. E. W. T. Richmond, manag- ing director of Messrs. Richmond gas stove manufac- turers, Warrington and London, and the Commercial Union Assurance Company (Limited), with reference to the X,.1,000 alleged to have become due on Mr. Richmond's death through a cycling accident in Guam-all last summer in circumstances already reported. Mr. Colan, Recorder of Croydon, instructed l)y. Mr. H. Hatton, of Messrs. Robert Davies and Co., of Warrington, and Mr. Edward Boase, of Penzanoe, appeared for the executors, and Mr. Ruegg, instructed by Mr. Payne, of Manchester, and Mr. G. L. B2dilly, of Penzance, appeared for th Company. The ease was referred to arbitration by Mr. Justice Bucknill in accordance with a condition endorsed on the policy. The company resisted the claim, alleging that Mr. Richmond was guilty of such gross carelessness in exposing himself to danger as to invalidate the policy. The first statement furnished to the company was to the effect that the deceased came to grief on Treen Hill, between Logan Rock and Penzance, in trying to , clear some vehicles, but this statement was withdrawn. The evidence given for the company before ' the arbitrator was to the effect. that Mr. Richmond rode down this hill, which was a dangerous one, although he knew that his front brake- was broken and his back brake inefficient. They called Mr. R. P. Couch, ex-Mayor of Penzance, who is consul of the Cyclists' Touring Club for Cornwall, who stated that he had carefully tested the back brake. The rubber of fibre blocks were worn very irregularly. On one side there was no sign of wear, as if that edge had never touched the rim. On the inner edge the block was worn down to the holder. On applying the brake the remaining rubber did not touch the wheel. With a heavy rider in the saddle he considered the brake would be quite useless. It was stated that deceased weighed 14st. For the executors, a policeman, who examined the bicycle after the aecident, said that although the front wheel wasbuckled back almost to the fork, the back, wheel was uninjured and the brake thoroughly effi- cient. A coastguard corroborated this, and statea that the hill WIIS regularly ridden without brakes, and no danger board had been placed there. By agreement the issue was narrowed down to the question whether or not the back brake was in good order, and whether deceased knew it ,would not hold and whether he should have ascertained the nature of the hill before his machine got beyond control.—Mr. Duke awarded the executors the full amount claimed, with costs of refer- ence and award.

It does not seem tome that the:ftction of the members of this wealthy trade in thus coming cap in hand to the ratepayers, adds either to their dignity or self-respect. Their claim has neither legal nor moral sanction, and they would be well advised if they abandoned it, and turned their attention to the developing of their insur- ance funds for compensating licensees whose license has not been renewed.
